However, **conversion to Non-B is not universally mandatory for foreigners holding Non-O status through marriage to a Thai citizen**.

### BOI Documentation Confirms the Distinction

The Board of Investment's Single Window guidance also expressly recognizes Non-O (Thai Spouse) alongside Non-B, Non-IB, and permanent-resident status for eligible applications under BOI employment privileges.[^214]

Its instructions impose additional immigration and eligibility conditions for applicants relying on Thai-spouse status.

That distinction reinforces the central point: the government does not require every qualifying foreign spouse to abandon Non-O status and obtain Non-B before pursuing employment authorization.

### Thai Visa Centre Contradicts Its Own Marriage-Visa Page

TVC's separate commercial marriage-visa page correctly describes the Non-Immigrant O marriage visa as providing work-permit eligibility.[^215]

Its FAQ confirms that a marriage-visa holder can work after obtaining the necessary work permit.

The two commercial pages therefore provide conflicting instructions:

- **90-day Non-Immigrant visa page:** Requires conversion to Non-B before employment.
- **Marriage-visa page:** Recognizes work-permit eligibility under the existing Non-O marriage category.

Both pages advertise professional visa assistance, including immigration-status changes and work-permit services.

### Consequences for Foreign Spouses

The incorrect instruction could lead eligible foreign spouses to believe they must change immigration categories before applying for employment authorization.

Unnecessary conversion could involve additional applications, government fees, document preparation, administrative delays, or complications with an existing marriage-based extension.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has unnecessarily converted customers' visas or charged fees for conversions that were not required.

Nevertheless, its published guidance incorrectly presents a category-specific employment procedure as a universal legal requirement.

TVC should distinguish employment authorization from visa classification, explain when Non-O marriage holders can obtain work permits, and reconcile its 90-day visa FAQ with its own marriage-visa service page.

## SMART Visa Service Advertises Categories Discontinued in 2025

Thai Visa Centre continues advertising Thailand's SMART Visa program as an immigration option for highly skilled professionals, investors, senior executives, and startup entrepreneurs.

However, **three of the four principal visa categories advertised on its commercial service page were discontinued for new applicants in February 2025**.[^169][^170]

The discrepancy is significant because TVC presents the outdated categories as currently available services and encourages prospective customers to begin applications.

### BOI Restricted New SMART Visas to Startup Entrepreneurs

On February 18, 2025, Thailand's Board of Investment issued Announcement No. Por 5/2568, following a Cabinet resolution dated January 13, 2025.[^170]

The government explicitly decided to retain SMART Visa eligibility for startup entrepreneurs while eliminating overlap with Thailand's Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa program.

The announcement repealed the previous SMART Visa qualification framework established in December 2018.

For new applications, it identifies only:

- **SMART S:** Qualifying entrepreneurs operating certified startups in Thailand.
- **SMART O:** Eligible spouses and children of those entrepreneurs.

The former SMART Talent (T), Investor (I), and Executive (E) categories are no longer available to new applicants under the revised framework.

The announcement specifies that the replacement rules apply to applications submitted after the change.

It does not establish that existing SMART Visa holders automatically lost their previously granted immigration status.

### TVC Continues Advertising Discontinued Categories

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand SMART Visa* service page nevertheless presents the program as a four-year immigration solution for professionals, investors, executives, and startup founders.[^169]

Its eligibility and application sections separately advertise:

- **SMART Talent (T):** Highly skilled professionals earning at least 100,000 baht monthly, with certain exceptions.
- **SMART Investor (I):** Investors committing 20 million baht to technology companies or 5 million baht to qualifying startups.
- **SMART Executive (E):** Senior executives earning at least 200,000 baht monthly.
- **SMART Startup (S):** Startup founders meeting investment, ownership, and financial requirements.

The page also advertises four-year stays, qualification endorsements, visa conversions, and government liaison services.

These descriptions reflect the former SMART Visa framework rather than the program established by the BOI's February 2025 announcement.

**TVC is advertising government visa categories that the responsible government agency no longer accepts for new applications.**

### The Four-Year Visa Claim Is Also Outdated

The distinction affects more than the names of the eligible categories.

TVC prominently describes SMART Visas as allowing stays of up to four years.

That duration was associated with certain former SMART Talent, Investor, and Executive categories.

The replacement announcement instead provides for qualifying startup entrepreneurs to receive permission for a period not exceeding two years, subject to the applicable requirements.[^170]

Consequently, **the four-year duration used to promote TVC's current SMART Visa services no longer describes the program available to new applicants under the revised rules**.

### Consequences for Prospective Customers

A professional or investor relying on TVC's page could spend time preparing financial evidence, employment contracts, investment documents, or qualification endorsements for a discontinued visa category.

The outdated information could also lead applicants to compare immigration options using benefits and stay durations that are no longer available.

The BOI's decision specifically sought to reduce overlap between SMART Visas and the LTR program, making accurate distinctions between those pathways particularly important.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has submitted applications under abolished categories, collected fees for unavailable visas, or caused a particular customer financial loss.

However, **the company's commercial website continues promoting discontinued immigration categories more than a year after the government formally restricted the program**.

Thai Visa Centre should update its advertised categories, remove obsolete four-year eligibility claims, and explain which immigration pathways remain available to professionals and investors affected by the 2025 changes.


## Tourist Visa Page Understates Financial Requirements and Misstates Insurance Rules

Thai Visa Centre's commercial tourist-visa page provides incorrect guidance concerning financial evidence and travel insurance.

The page identifies minimum funds of **10,000 baht per individual or 20,000 baht per family**, while Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs specifies **20,000 baht per individual or 40,000 baht per family** in its general tourist-visa requirements.[^171][^172]

The same service page also presents valid travel insurance as mandatory, despite the absence of a universal insurance requirement for standard tourist visas.

### Published Financial Requirements Are Half the Official Amounts

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Tourist Visa* page identifies the following requirements for single-entry tourist visas:

- Bank statements showing at least 10,000 baht per person.
- Bank statements showing at least 20,000 baht per family.

The amounts appear in both its visa-category checklist and general financial requirements.[^171]

However, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs identifies adequate financial evidence for tourist-visa applications as **20,000 baht per person and 40,000 baht per family**.[^172]

The Royal Thai Embassy in Budapest independently confirms these amounts in its February 2026 tourist-visa guidance.[^173]

The discrepancy is significant because TVC's figures match the lower amounts published for Thailand's separate Visa on Arrival scheme, rather than the standard tourist visa.[^174]

**TVC appears to have applied Visa on Arrival financial thresholds to a different immigration category.**

Although individual embassies may impose additional documentary or financial requirements, this does not support advertising a lower general threshold than the Ministry of Foreign Affairs specifies.

An applicant relying on TVC's figures could prepare inadequate financial evidence and encounter requests for additional documentation or an unsuccessful application.

### Insurance Presented as a Mandatory Tourist-Visa Condition

The same page lists the following restriction:

> Must maintain valid travel insurance

However, standard Thai tourist visas do not carry a general government requirement to maintain travel insurance throughout the stay.

The Ministry of Foreign Affairs' published tourist-visa documentation requirements do not identify insurance as a universal prerequisite.[^172]

More significantly, Thai Visa Centre's own *Do I Need Health Insurance for Thailand?* FAQ acknowledges that standard tourist visas do not generally require mandatory health insurance.[^175]

That article recommends obtaining insurance for medical expenses, evacuation, and other travel risks.

**Recommending insurance is materially different from presenting it as a compulsory immigration condition.**

Specific diplomatic missions may request additional documentation in individual circumstances. The available evidence does not establish that every tourist-visa application is exempt from every possible insurance-related request.

Nevertheless, TVC's categorical requirement is inconsistent with both the general government guidance and its own published explanation.

### Implications for Applicants

These errors concern two basic questions applicants must resolve before paying for professional visa assistance:

1. How much financial evidence must they prepare?
2. Is purchasing travel insurance compulsory or optional?

TVC's financial guidance could leave customers underprepared, while its insurance statement could lead customers to believe they must purchase coverage to satisfy an immigration requirement that does not generally exist.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has sold unnecessary insurance, caused visa refusals, or submitted applications with insufficient financial evidence.

It establishes that **the agency's commercial tourist-visa page contains financial thresholds inconsistent with Ministry of Foreign Affairs requirements and an insurance obligation contradicted by its own guidance**.

Thai Visa Centre should correct its tourist-visa financial checklist, distinguish tourist visas from Visa on Arrival requirements, and explain insurance as a recommendation except where a specific applicable rule requires it.


## One-Year Visa Page Incorrectly Requires 90-Day Reports After Re-Entry

Thai Visa Centre's commercial *Thailand One-Year Non-Immigrant Visa* page incorrectly instructs frequent international travelers to continue filing 90-day immigration reports even when they regularly leave and re-enter Thailand.[^176]

This contradicts both the Immigration Bureau's official reporting rules and the agency's own published guidance.

**The discrepancy is particularly relevant because Thai Visa Centre also sells paid 90-day reporting assistance through an affiliated service.**

### TVC Claims Reporting Remains Mandatory

Thai Visa Centre advertises its one-year multiple-entry Non-Immigrant Visa as suitable for foreigners making frequent visits to Thailand.

The service page explains that the visa generally allows stays of up to 90 days per entry.

Under its FAQ, however, the company answers the question *What about 90-day reporting?* by stating:

> Yes, you must still report to immigration every 90 days, even if you regularly leave and re-enter Thailand.

Its restrictions section also lists 90-day reporting as mandatory, without explaining that the obligation depends on the length of continuous residence.[^176]

**This incorrectly suggests that the reporting schedule continues regardless of international departures and subsequent re-entry.**

### Immigration Bureau Rules Reset the Reporting Clock

Thailand's official government guidance, sourced from Immigration Division 1, explains that foreigners staying in Thailand for more than 90 days must notify immigration of their residence.[^177]

Crucially, it states:

> If a foreigner leaves the Kingdom and re-enters, the day count starts at 0 in every case.

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles independently publishes the same rule.[^178]

Consequently, a foreigner who leaves Thailand before reaching the reporting deadline does not need to submit a report for that interrupted period.

The 90-day count begins again following the next entry.

A traveler making repeated visits shorter than 90 consecutive days may therefore never become obligated to file a TM47 report during those visits.

This does not eliminate reporting requirements for foreigners who remain in Thailand continuously for the relevant period, or excuse violations that occurred before departure.

### TVC's Own FAQ Contradicts Its Service Page

The inconsistency is especially clear because Thai Visa Centre publishes a separate FAQ titled *Do I Need to Do 90-Day Reporting While Outside of Thailand?*[^179]

That article correctly explains that:

- No report is required for a reporting period interrupted by departure before its deadline.
- Each re-entry restarts the 90-day count.
- Reporting resumes only after the applicable continuous-stay period.

**The agency therefore gives customers opposite answers about the same immigration obligation, depending on which page they consult.**

Both publications promote TVC's immigration assistance.

### Relevance to Paid Reporting Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises paid 90-day reporting assistance, including through its affiliated `90day.in.th` service.

Customers following the one-year visa page could incorrectly believe that they must purchase reporting assistance despite regularly departing Thailand before reaching the reporting threshold.

There is no evidence that TVC has charged a customer for an unnecessary report or deliberately misrepresented the rule to generate business.

However, **its commercial service page incorrectly presents a conditional immigration obligation as mandatory for travelers whose repeated departures may eliminate the need to report altogether**.

The agency should correct the FAQ, distinguish continuous residence from repeated short visits, and reconcile its commercial visa guidance with its own accurate explanation of the reporting clock.


## Five-Year Retirement Visa Page Advertises Work Rights Contrary to Official Restrictions

Thai Visa Centre advertises Thailand's Non-Immigrant O-X retirement visa as a long-term immigration option for qualifying foreigners aged 50 and older.

However, its commercial service page promotes remote employment and volunteer activities as benefits of the visa, despite official Thai diplomatic guidance expressly prohibiting employment under the O-X category.[^180][^181]

**The agency markets work-related benefits while simultaneously acknowledging that O-X holders are prohibited from employment.**

### TVC Promises Remote Work and Volunteer Opportunities

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand 5-Year Retirement Visa* page lists the following benefits:

- Remote work permitted.
- Volunteer work options.

Its frequently asked questions reinforce those claims.

When asked whether O-X holders can work, TVC initially acknowledges that employment is prohibited.

However, it immediately states:

> However, you can work remotely for foreign companies and volunteer for approved activities.

The same page identifies the visa as a retirement category and lists employment in Thailand as a restriction.[^180]

**These statements create the impression that working remotely for overseas employers is an established exception to the visa's employment restrictions.**

### Thai Diplomatic Missions State That Employment Is Prohibited

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles publishes official O-X guidance, updated October 1, 2026.[^181]

It identifies the visa as intended for qualifying foreigners aged 50 and older who wish to reside in Thailand without undertaking employment.

The guidance states:

> Employment of any kind is strictly prohibited.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Kuala Lumpur independently repeats that restriction in its O-X instructions, updated July 17, 2026.[^182]

Neither publication identifies a general exemption allowing O-X holders to undertake remote employment for foreign companies.

The distinction matters because an employer's location outside Thailand does not, by itself, establish that work performed while physically present in Thailand is authorized under a particular immigration category.

**TVC presents remote employment as an affirmative visa benefit without identifying any government authorization supporting that claim.**

### Volunteering Requires Separate Consideration

The agency's reference to volunteer opportunities introduces a related concern.

Unpaid activity is not automatically exempt from Thailand's rules governing work by foreign nationals.

Whether a particular volunteer arrangement requires permission depends on the activity and applicable immigration and employment regulations.

TVC does not identify which volunteer activities it considers permissible, what authorization may be required, or how those activities would remain consistent with the O-X visa's restrictions.

Its broad marketing language therefore risks implying that volunteering is an ordinary benefit of retirement status rather than an activity requiring separate legal assessment.

### Consequences for Retirement Applicants

Customers considering the O-X visa must satisfy substantial financial and insurance requirements.

The visa generally requires a qualifying Thai bank deposit of at least 3 million baht, or a qualifying combination of deposits and annual income.

An applicant who intends to continue remote employment may view TVC's advertised work rights as an important reason to select this expensive long-stay arrangement.

However, **the government's published O-X conditions do not support presenting remote employment as an unrestricted benefit of the visa**.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has arranged unauthorized employment, advised a particular customer to violate work restrictions, or caused an immigration violation.

It establishes that the agency's commercial service page promotes work-related activities while failing to reconcile those claims with the explicit employment prohibition published by Thai diplomatic missions.

Thai Visa Centre should remove the unqualified remote-work claim, clarify the separate rules governing volunteer activities, and distinguish retirement status from visa categories specifically intended for qualifying employment or remote work.


## Full-Refund Guarantee Conflicts With Published Refund Exclusions

Thai Visa Centre prominently advertises a refundable-deposit model intended to reassure customers paying in advance for immigration services.

Its homepage promises that customers receive a **full refund of their deposit if the agency does not complete the agreed service**.[^183]

However, the company's separately published Refund Policy allows certain payments to be retained, including government application fees following submission and transaction charges of 2–7% in specified circumstances.[^184]

**The public documents do not clearly explain how these deductions operate alongside the company's advertised full-refund guarantee.**

### Homepage Promises Full Refunds for Uncompleted Services

Under *Transparent payments, refundable deposits, and nationwide service*, Thai Visa Centre states that all visa-service payments are accepted as refundable deposits under written agreements.

It explains that successful completion allows the company to retain the deposit as agreed.

For unsuccessful services, the homepage promises:

> If we do not complete the service as agreed, you receive a full refund of your deposit.

The company also states that refund terms may vary slightly by service but that every customer receives a written agreement before work begins.[^183]

This language presents the refundable-deposit arrangement as a general customer protection rather than an exception available to selected visa categories.

### Published Policy Excludes Certain Payments

Thai Visa Centre's Refund Policy, updated February 9, 2025, establishes more restrictive conditions.[^184]

For applications denied after submission, it distinguishes between two categories of charges:

- **Government application fees:** Nonrefundable once submitted to the relevant embassy or consulate.
- **Agency service fees:** Advertised as fully refundable when the visa application is unsuccessful.

The policy also permits deductions of 2–7% for transaction charges in certain refund scenarios, including cancellations requested after its 12-hour period.

Consequently, a customer whose visa application fails may receive their agency service fee without recovering the entire amount originally paid.

That distinction is not explained alongside the homepage's general promise of a full deposit refund.

### The Meaning of Service Completion Remains Unclear

A further issue concerns what the company considers successful completion of an immigration service.

The homepage ties refund eligibility to whether Thai Visa Centre completes the service *as agreed*.

The Refund Policy instead distinguishes between applications submitted to government authorities and applications approved or denied.

These are not necessarily equivalent outcomes.

For example, an agency might fulfill a document-preparation or submission obligation even when immigration authorities subsequently refuse the application.

Alternatively, a customer's agreement might expressly make approval a condition of successful service delivery.

**TVC does not publicly reconcile these distinctions or explain which definition governs its broad homepage guarantee.**

The separately advertised written deposit agreements might contain additional protections or exceptions. Their individual terms have not been examined in this investigation.

### Implications for Customers Paying in Advance

Immigration applications can involve substantial agency fees, government charges, and additional expenses for supporting documentation.

Customers deciding whether to pay upfront may reasonably consider refund protection an important factor.

The distinction between receiving a full deposit refund and receiving only the refundable agency-fee component is therefore financially significant.

Thai Visa Centre's Terms & Conditions introduce further uncertainty by identifying the contracting party only as THAI VISA CENTRE and imposing broad limitations of financial liability.[^185]

The existing investigation separately examines those contractual provisions.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has unlawfully withheld refunds, failed to honor a customer's individual agreement, or deliberately misrepresented its payment protections.

However, **its prominent full-refund marketing is not clearly reconciled with the narrower refund entitlements and permitted deductions described in its published policy**.

Thai Visa Centre should explain whether its refundable-deposit guarantee includes government charges, identify when transaction fees may be deducted, clarify what constitutes unsuccessful service delivery, and provide consistent refund information before customers make payments.


## Thailand Privilege Service Page Incorrectly Claims Stay Extensions Are Unnecessary

Thai Visa Centre advertises Thailand Privilege memberships costing between 650,000 and 5 million baht, offering immigration arrangements lasting five to twenty years.

However, its commercial Thailand Privilege Visa page incorrectly tells customers that stay extensions are unnecessary.[^186]

**This contradicts the official program operator's published requirements for members remaining continuously in Thailand beyond their initial permission to stay.**

### TVC Advertises No Extension Requirement

Under its visa-validity information, Thai Visa Centre identifies:

- Membership duration: 5–20 years.
- Permitted stay: One year per entry.
- Extensions: "No extensions needed - multiple re-entry permitted."

The presentation suggests that the program's multiple-entry privileges eliminate the need for immigration extensions.[^186]

However, these are separate matters.

A Thailand Privilege membership may remain valid for many years, but the permission granted upon a particular entry is generally limited to one year.

Multiple-entry privileges allow members to depart and return during their visa's validity. They do not automatically extend an existing period of uninterrupted residence.

### Official Operator Requires Extensions for Continuous Stays

Thailand Privilege Card Co., Ltd. publishes explicit instructions for members seeking to remain beyond their permitted stay.[^187]

Its official guidance explains that members can apply for an extension at the immigration office responsible for their residence.

The procedure requires:

- An application using immigration Form TM7.
- Supporting passport and membership documentation.
- Personal attendance for photographs and fingerprints.
- A government extension fee of 1,900 baht.

Applications may generally be submitted up to 30 days before the current permission expires.

The operator also confirms that Privilege Entry Visa holders can receive a one-year extension with each successful application.

**Members wishing to remain continuously in Thailand beyond their current permitted period therefore cannot rely solely on the remaining validity of their five- to twenty-year membership.**

### TVC's Own Guide Acknowledges the Requirement

Thai Visa Centre's separate guide, *Thailand Privilege Visa: Tiers, Benefits, Eligibility, and Application 2026*, correctly distinguishes membership validity from permitted stay.[^188]

It explains that members receive up to one year per entry and must either:

- Depart and re-enter to receive a new period of permission, subject to immigration approval; or
- Apply for a one-year extension inside Thailand.

This directly conflicts with the categorical statement on TVC's commercial service page that no extensions are needed.

**The company therefore publishes accurate instructions in one article while advertising a more convenient but incomplete account of the same immigration obligations on its sales page.**

### Consequences for Long-Term Residents

Thailand Privilege is marketed specifically to foreigners seeking convenient long-term residence without the financial-document requirements associated with retirement visas.

Customers may reasonably distinguish these expensive memberships from arrangements involving frequent immigration renewals.

However, members intending to remain continuously in Thailand must still monitor their permission-to-stay expiration dates and obtain extensions when necessary.

Failing to do so can result in overstay, even while the underlying Thailand Privilege membership remains valid.

Members who instead depart and re-enter before their permission expires may avoid the domestic extension process, but international travel is not equivalent to an automatic extension.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has caused customers to overstay or incorrectly processed membership extensions.

Nevertheless, **its commercial website misrepresents an important ongoing immigration obligation associated with memberships costing hundreds of thousands or millions of baht**.

Thai Visa Centre should correct the service page, distinguish membership validity from each entry's permitted stay, and explain the available extension and re-entry procedures consistently across its publications.


## LTR Visa Sales Page Retains Eligibility Requirements Abolished in 2025

Thai Visa Centre's commercial Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa page continues imposing financial and employment-history requirements removed by Thailand's Board of Investment (BOI) in February 2025.[^197]

The discrepancies affect wealthy investors, remote workers, and highly skilled professionals.

**More significantly, TVC has published a separate guide correctly explaining these reforms, demonstrating that its own immigration guidance contradicts its commercial eligibility checklist.**

### Sales Page Requires Income No Longer Mandatory for Wealthy Investors

Under *Wealthy Global Citizens*, TVC lists personal income of at least **USD 80,000 annually for the preceding two years** as a required qualification.[^197]

However, BOI Announcement Por. 3/2568, effective February 4, 2025, abolished that income requirement.[^198]

Applicants must still demonstrate:

- At least USD 1 million in qualifying assets.
- At least USD 500,000 in qualifying Thai investments.
- Compliance with the applicable healthcare and immigration requirements.

The reform removed the personal-income test, not the underlying wealth and investment thresholds.

**A financially qualified investor could incorrectly conclude that they are ineligible solely because their annual income falls below USD 80,000.**

### Five-Year Work Experience Requirements Were Also Removed

TVC's same service page requires:

- Five years of relevant work experience for Work-from-Thailand Professionals.
- Five years of experience in targeted industries for Highly Skilled Professionals.[^197]

Both requirements were removed under the 2025 reforms.

The BOI's official announcement confirms that the government eliminated the minimum five-year experience requirement for these two categories.[^199]

Applicants must still satisfy their category's applicable income, employment, employer, or professional qualifications.

For example, Work-from-Thailand Professionals generally need qualifying overseas employment and must meet the relevant personal-income threshold.

**The absence of five years' employment history is no longer, by itself, grounds to exclude an otherwise qualified applicant.**

### TVC's Own 2025 Guide Explains the Changes Correctly

The most significant contradiction is internal.

Thai Visa Centre publishes a separate article titled *LTR Visa Thailand Eases Eligibility Requirements: What Changed in 2025 and Who Benefits*.[^200]

That article correctly explains:

1. Wealthy Global Citizens no longer need USD 80,000 in annual personal income.
2. Five-year experience requirements were removed for Work-from-Thailand and Highly Skilled Professionals.
3. The qualifying overseas-employer revenue threshold was reduced from USD 150 million to USD 50 million over three years.
4. Dependent eligibility was expanded under the revised framework.

It identifies February 4, 2025, as the effective date and refers to the same official BOI announcement.

**TVC therefore possesses and publishes accurate information about the reforms but has not reconciled that information with the eligibility criteria on its main commercial service page.**

### Consequences for Potential Applicants

LTR visas offer up to ten years of residence, subject to continuing qualification checks, along with immigration and tax privileges.

Applicants often require substantial financial documentation and professional assistance to establish eligibility.

Incorrect eligibility requirements can discourage legitimate applicants from applying, lead to unnecessary document preparation, or produce inaccurate assessments before a customer pays for services.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has rejected qualified customers, collected unnecessary fees, or knowingly misrepresented an applicant's eligibility.

However, **its commercial eligibility checklist continues to enforce requirements that the agency itself correctly describes as abolished more than a year earlier**.

Thai Visa Centre should update the LTR service page to reflect BOI Announcement Por. 3/2568, distinguish current requirements from historical criteria, and reconcile its sales materials with its own accurate reform guide.

### LTR Sales Page Misrepresents Application Fees and Payment Timing

Thai Visa Centre's commercial LTR visa page lists a government fee of **50,000 baht per person**, describing it as an application fee that is non-refundable if the application is denied.[^205]

This presentation fails to distinguish the free initial qualification process from the subsequent paid visa-issuance stage.

#### BOI Confirms Applications Are Free Until Approval

Thailand's Board of Investment explicitly states that LTR applications and qualification processing are free of charge.[^206]

The official procedure separates:

1. **Qualification endorsement:** Applicants submit supporting documents for government assessment without paying an application fee to the BOI.
2. **Visa-issuance pre-approval:** Approved applicants complete additional immigration requirements.
3. **Visa issuance:** Applicants pay the applicable government fee when proceeding with issuance.

For applicants collecting their LTR visa at the Thailand Investment and Expat Services Center in Bangkok, the government charges 50,000 baht after approval.[^207]

The fee is therefore not an upfront payment required merely to have LTR eligibility assessed.

#### Overseas Visa Fees Are Not Universally Fixed

TVC additionally presents the 50,000-baht amount as a fixed embassy-fee range.

The BOI explains that overseas embassy, consulate, and e-Visa charges can differ from the Bangkok fee because of exchange rates and additional consular charges.[^208]

Importantly, official guidance confirms that e-Visa processing fees are non-refundable and that applicants may need to pay again if an e-Visa application is rejected because of incorrect information.[^207]

Non-refundable fees can therefore arise at the later visa-issuance stage.

**That does not justify presenting the initial BOI qualification application itself as a non-refundable 50,000-baht transaction.**

#### Financial Implications for Applicants

The distinction is significant for applicants uncertain whether they satisfy LTR eligibility requirements.

TVC's wording could lead prospective customers to believe that they must risk 50,000 baht in government fees simply to obtain an initial eligibility decision.

In reality, the BOI provides that assessment without charging an application fee.

Private agents may separately charge for document preparation or representation, but those commercial fees must not be confused with official government charges.

There is no evidence that TVC has actually collected the 50,000-baht government fee prematurely or withheld refunds from unsuccessful applicants.

Nevertheless, its published financial guidance obscures when government payment becomes necessary and the circumstances in which a non-refundable charge may arise.

TVC should distinguish free BOI qualification processing from paid visa issuance, identify the applicable overseas fee variations, and explain which payments are genuinely at risk if an application fails.

### LTR Dependent Expansion Advertised Despite Unresolved Immigration Rules

Thai Visa Centre's guide *LTR Visa Thailand Eases Eligibility Requirements*, describing the February 2025 reforms, tells prospective customers that parents and other legal dependents can now obtain LTR visas without a numerical limit.[^216]

It presents these changes as effective from February 4, 2025, and instructs families to submit applications under the expanded rules.

However, the BOI's current official LTR website continues publishing narrower dependent-visa requirements, creating a significant discrepancy between TVC's assurances and the government's operational guidance.[^217]

#### BOI Reform Required Separate Ministry of Interior Action

On January 13, 2025, Thailand's Cabinet approved expanding LTR dependent eligibility to include parents and other legal dependents while removing the four-person limit.

The BOI announced the proposed expansion but explicitly stated that implementation depended on both its updated eligibility announcement and a separate Ministry of Interior announcement addressing dependent rights.[^218]

BOI Announcement Por. 3/2568, effective February 4, 2025, subsequently incorporated parents and legal dependents into its eligibility criteria.[^198]

However, the existing Ministry of Interior announcement governing LTR permission to stay limits dependents to legal spouses and children under 20, with a maximum of four accompanying persons.[^219]

The BOI's current public eligibility page still reproduces those restrictions.[^217]

**TVC therefore presents the expanded criteria as fully operational without explaining the discrepancy between BOI qualification rules and the published immigration-stay requirements.**

#### Thai Visa Centre's Family Guide Contradicts Its Reform Announcement

TVC's separate *LTR Visa Thailand for Spouses and Dependents* guide provides substantially different advice.[^220]

It states that parents and other relatives generally do not qualify under standard dependent rules unless the policy explicitly expands, advising customers to confirm eligibility with the BOI.

This directly conflicts with its 2025 reforms guide, which states that parents and other legal dependents can already apply without a fixed limit.[^216]

The two publications therefore differ on a fundamental question: whether expanded family eligibility is an established application entitlement or a policy change requiring further confirmation.

#### Consequences for Families

The discrepancy could affect applicants intending to relocate with elderly parents, legally dependent relatives, or more than four family members.

Families may incur costs for translations, insurance, supporting documentation, professional services, and travel planning before discovering that the relevant immigration authorities apply narrower dependent rules.

The evidence does not establish that TVC has submitted unsuccessful applications under the expanded categories or collected fees from affected families.

It also does not conclusively establish that the Ministry of Interior has never issued a subsequent implementing measure.

However, the official BOI website continues displaying the older restrictions, and its published regulatory materials do not reconcile those restrictions with the expanded 2025 qualifications.

TVC should identify the operative Ministry of Interior authority permitting expanded dependent stays, if one exists, and distinguish approved BOI eligibility changes from immigration permissions actually available to applicants.

Until that discrepancy is resolved, advertising unrestricted family eligibility as an established entitlement is potentially misleading.

## Citizenship Guidance Misrepresents Permanent Residency Requirements for Foreign Spouses

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *Do I Get Thai Nationality After Marriage?*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents permanent residency as a necessary stage before citizenship for foreign spouses.[^201]

Under its *Marriage and naturalisation* comparison, the agency labels the proposition "I can skip PR if I am married to a Thai" as false.

Its proposed citizenship timeline similarly requires applicants to obtain permanent residency before applying for naturalization. The page also warns against applying without PR or sufficient residency history.

**These instructions fail to distinguish the separate citizenship routes established under Thailand's Nationality Act.**

### Foreign Wives Have a Separate Citizenship Procedure

Section 9 of the Nationality Act B.E. 2508 (1965) permits a foreign woman married to a Thai citizen to apply for Thai nationality through a procedure distinct from ordinary naturalization.[^202]

The Department of Provincial Administration's published criteria include:

- A legally registered marriage of at least three years, reduced to one year where the couple has a child together.
- Domicile and household-registration documentation.
- Applicable spousal income and tax evidence.
- Marriage verification, background screening, and official approval.[^203]

These requirements do not establish prior permanent-resident status as a universal prerequisite.

TVC's blanket rejection of the possibility of bypassing PR therefore misrepresents an available statutory route.

### Foreign Husbands Receive Different Statutory Exemptions

Section 11(4), introduced in 2008, separately addresses foreign husbands of Thai citizens.

It expressly exempts qualifying applicants from two ordinary naturalization requirements under Section 10:

1. Five consecutive years of domicile under Section 10(4).
2. The statutory Thai-language requirement under Section 10(5).[^202]

Department of Provincial Administration guidance also recognizes applications by foreign husbands after three years of registered marriage, or one year where the couple has a child together.[^204]

Other qualifications, lawful-residence documentation, financial evidence, administrative screening, and discretionary approval remain relevant.

**TVC's generalized requirements for permanent residency and Thai-language testing obscure these statutory exemptions.**

### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Paid Services

Thai Visa Centre separately markets professional assistance with permanent-residency applications.[^193]

Presenting PR as an unavoidable prerequisite for citizenship could encourage eligible spouses to pursue an additional immigration process involving substantial fees, documentation, and delays.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC deliberately misdirected customers or collected unnecessary fees.

Nevertheless, its published guidance fails to explain legally significant alternatives that could materially affect applicants' decisions.

TVC should distinguish ordinary naturalization from nationality acquisition under Section 9 and the exemptions under Section 11, rather than presenting permanent residency as a standard compulsory step for foreign spouses.

## Thai Nationality FAQ Incorrectly Excludes Children of Foreign Parents

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *Thai Nationality by Birth – Who Qualifies?*, marked reviewed June 2026, categorically states in its comparison table that children born in Thailand to two foreign parents do not acquire Thai nationality.[^311]

The guide repeatedly explains that children of foreign parents remain foreign nationals, generally inheriting their parents' citizenship.

Although its introduction briefly acknowledges unspecified exceptions, the detailed guidance does not identify the circumstances in which Thailand-born children of foreign nationals may acquire Thai citizenship.

**This oversimplification omits important statutory nationality rights and could mislead families about their children's legal status.**

### Nationality Act Recognizes Birthright Citizenship in Certain Cases

Section 7(2) of Thailand's Nationality Act B.E. 2508 (1965) provides for Thai nationality by birth to persons born in Thailand, subject to statutory exclusions.[^312]

Section 7 bis excludes children born to foreign parents where a parent holds certain temporary immigration permissions, receives special temporary residence concessions, or resides unlawfully.

Section 8 separately excludes specified diplomatic, consular, and international-organization personnel and their families.

These provisions do not categorically exclude every child born to two foreign nationals.

**A child born in Thailand whose foreign parents both hold qualifying permanent-resident status may acquire Thai nationality by birth under Section 7(2).**

The parents' immigration status at the time of birth, relevant statutory exclusions, and official civil-registration records determine the applicable legal position.

Children born to tourists or other temporary residents generally do not acquire Thai nationality merely because they were born in Thailand.

TVC's unconditional comparison table fails to explain this distinction.

### Other Statutory Routes Exist for Thailand-Born Children

Section 7 bis also permits the Minister of Interior to grant Thai nationality in qualifying cases under Cabinet-established criteria.[^312]

Separately, Section 23 of the Nationality Act provides a nationality procedure for certain people born in Thailand during specified historical periods, including individuals affected by earlier nationality restrictions.

Thailand's official government website identifies eligible groups, including people born between December 14, 1972, and February 25, 1992, under particular parental immigration circumstances, and certain qualifying descendants.[^313]

These are conditional statutory procedures, not automatic citizenship entitlements for every child of foreign parents.

Nevertheless, they contradict the impression that having two foreign parents necessarily rules out Thai nationality.

### Consequences for Foreign Families

Thai Visa Centre advertises assistance with birth registration, family immigration documentation, and dependent visas.

Families relying on its simplified nationality advice could fail to investigate whether their children already possess Thai nationality by birth or qualify under another statutory procedure.

Potential consequences include:

- Unnecessary applications for foreign passports or dependent immigration status.
- Failure to pursue appropriate Thai civil-registration documentation.
- Delayed recognition of citizenship or nationality eligibility.
- Additional legal expenses to correct misunderstandings about a child's status.

Nationality and immigration status can affect education, residence, identification documents, and access to public services.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has caused a particular child to lose citizenship rights or incur financial losses through this FAQ.

Nevertheless, **the agency presents an incomplete explanation of nationality law while marketing professional assistance to foreign families.**

TVC should revise its FAQ to distinguish children of temporary foreign residents from children whose parents hold qualifying permanent residence, explain the relevant statutory exceptions, and direct families to the Department of Provincial Administration for individual nationality determinations.

## Adoption Guides Incorrectly Require Thai Family Court Orders

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Adoption Overview*, marked reviewed June 2026, identifies Thai family courts as authorities responsible for issuing adoption orders and claims that prospective parents must follow court procedures.[^324]

Its separate *Thailand Adoption Requirements* guide presents a family-court petition, hearing, judgment, and subsequent registration as mandatory stages of the adoption process.[^325]

A third publication, *Child Adoption in Thailand*, states that domestic adoptions require family-court orders and repeatedly describes licensed attorneys and court proceedings as necessary.[^326]

**These categorical instructions contradict Thailand's statutory adoption framework and official government procedures.**

### Civil and Commercial Code Establishes Registration as the Legal Requirement

Section 1598/27 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code provides that an adoption becomes legally effective upon registration according to law.[^327]

For minors, the statutory process must also comply with Thailand's Child Adoption Act and applicable administrative requirements.

Ordinary adoption procedures therefore involve approval by the appropriate child-adoption authorities and subsequent registration, rather than a universal requirement to obtain a Thai family-court judgment.

Thailand's official government website confirms that:

- Adoption of a minor ordinarily requires Child Adoption Board approval.
- Following approval, applicants register the adoption with the appropriate registrar.
- Qualifying adult adoptions may be registered without Child Adoption Board approval.[^328]

Court intervention may be necessary in exceptional circumstances, including disputes concerning parental consent or other statutory requirements.

However, **the existence of special judicial procedures does not establish that all adoptions require court orders.**

TVC's guides fail to distinguish these situations.

### U.S. State Department Explicitly Confirms Thai Courts Are Not Involved

The discrepancy is particularly clear for intercountry adoptions.

The U.S. Department of State's official *Thailand Intercountry Adoption Information* directly addresses the role of Thai courts.

It states that Thai courts are not involved in the ordinary intercountry adoption process.[^329]

Instead, Thailand's Department of Children and Youth and Child Adoption Board administer the applicable approval procedures.

Prospective adoptive parents may complete the supervised pre-adoption period and subsequently:

1. Obtain approval to finalize the adoption in the receiving country, where applicable.
2. Complete the placement period in Thailand and register the adoption through a Thai district office.

The Department of State also explains that the six-month supervised placement period may occur outside Thailand, subject to the applicable procedures and reporting requirements.

This contradicts TVC's presentation of a six-month placement in Thailand followed by a Thai family-court hearing as the standard intercountry adoption pathway.

**TVC incorrectly substitutes a judicial process for the administrative procedures expressly described by the relevant government authorities.**

### Thai Embassy Confirms Registration Through Diplomatic Missions

The Royal Thai Embassy in Washington independently describes the country's adoption process.[^330]

Its guidance identifies the Department of Social Development and Welfare, Child Adoption Board, supervised placement, and official registration as the principal stages.

After the applicable approvals, adoption registration can occur at:

- A Royal Thai Embassy or Consulate in the adoptive parents' country of residence.
- An authorized district or local registration office in Thailand.

The embassy does not describe a Thai family-court judgment as a universal requirement for these ordinary adoption procedures.

The distinction also matters for families completing adoptions abroad under the Hague Convention.

Such families may have separate judicial or administrative requirements in the receiving country, but those requirements should not be confused with a supposedly mandatory Thai family-court proceeding.

### TVC Repeatedly Promotes Legal Referrals for an Incorrectly Described Process

The problem extends beyond a single inaccurate checklist.

TVC's adoption publications repeatedly emphasize that prospective parents need licensed adoption lawyers and court proceedings.

Its *Child Adoption in Thailand* guide advertises referrals to specialist Thai counsel while describing court orders as mandatory for lawful adoption.

Qualified legal advice can be valuable, especially in complex family circumstances or disputed adoptions.

However, **the ordinary administrative adoption process should not be represented as universally requiring litigation or a Thai family-court order.**

Mischaracterizing the procedure could cause prospective adoptive parents to:

- Expect unnecessary court petitions and judicial hearings.
- Budget for legal representation based on an incorrect description of mandatory procedures.
- Misunderstand the role of the Child Adoption Board and Department of Children and Youth.
- Arrange extended stays in Thailand under the mistaken belief that supervised placement must occur locally.
- Delay lawful adoption planning while preparing for court proceedings that may not be required.

Intercountry adoption remains a heavily regulated process. Official approval, accredited adoption-service providers where applicable, child-welfare assessments, and receiving-country requirements must still be satisfied.

The absence of a mandatory Thai court hearing does not eliminate these safeguards.

### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination, document legalization, and referrals to specialist lawyers for prospective adoptive families.

The available evidence does not establish that the company has charged customers for unnecessary court proceedings, referred families to lawyers for nonexistent requirements, or caused failed adoptions.

Nevertheless, its published guidance repeatedly identifies judicial procedures as mandatory where official sources describe an administrative registration process.

**TVC should correct its adoption guides to distinguish Child Adoption Board approval, statutory registration, receiving-country requirements, and the exceptional circumstances in which Thai court intervention may actually be necessary.**

## Business-Formation Guide Presents Prohibited Nominee Shareholding as an Option

Thai Visa Centre's guide *Types of Business in Thailand: FBA Lists and Foreign Ownership*, marked reviewed June 2026, identifies Thai-majority limited companies as a standard structure involving genuine Thai partners or nominee arrangements reviewed by corporate counsel.[^225]

Its section on common mistakes separately warns against using nominee Thai shareholders **without documented control structures reviewed by corporate counsel**.

These statements suggest that nominee arrangements can be acceptable when documented or reviewed by lawyers.

However, Thailand's Foreign Business Act expressly prohibits nominee shareholding used to circumvent restrictions on foreign business ownership.

### Foreign Business Act Section 36 Prohibits Nominee Arrangements

Section 36 of the Foreign Business Act B.E. 2542 (1999) prohibits Thai nationals and qualifying legal entities from holding shares on behalf of foreigners to facilitate businesses that foreigners are not legally permitted to operate.[^226]

The prohibition also covers arrangements disguising foreign participation behind purportedly Thai-owned businesses.

Violations can result in imprisonment, substantial fines, and court orders terminating the prohibited arrangements.

Legitimate Thai-majority companies with genuine Thai shareholders are permitted. The legal problem arises when Thai shareholders merely represent foreign beneficial owners to evade statutory restrictions.

**Legal advice and shareholder documentation do not convert prohibited nominee arrangements into lawful ownership structures.**

### TVC's Other Guides Explicitly Warn Against Nominees

The inconsistency is particularly conspicuous because Thai Visa Centre correctly explains the prohibition elsewhere.

Its *Doing Business in Thailand* guide identifies nominee Thai shareholders as illegal and high-risk under Thai law.[^227]

Its separate property-ownership guide also warns that nominee companies used to conceal foreign land ownership are unlawful.[^228]

These publications contradict the business-structures guide's presentation of lawyer-reviewed nominee arrangements as an available corporate structure.

The company therefore publishes opposing explanations of a fundamental restriction under Thai business law.

### Relevance to TVC's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises business-visa assistance, work-permit coordination, corporate-document preparation, and referrals to company-registration advisers.

Foreign entrepreneurs relying on its guidance could misunderstand the distinction between genuine Thai-majority ownership and prohibited nominee arrangements.

That misunderstanding could expose applicants to registration problems, licensing disputes, criminal penalties, and financial losses.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has created nominee companies, recommended unlawful arrangements to specific customers, or facilitated violations of the Foreign Business Act.

Nevertheless, **its published business-formation guidance presents an explicitly prohibited ownership arrangement as though professional legal review could make it an acceptable option**.

TVC should remove nominee arrangements from its list of standard company structures, explain the prohibition under Section 36, and reconcile its business guidance with the warnings already published elsewhere on its website.

## Company Registration Guides Misstate Shareholder Requirements

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Limited Company Registration* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, states that standard private limited companies require three shareholders. It also claims that one-person companies are possible under recent reforms.[^291]

Both assertions conflict with Thailand's current company law.

### The 2023 Amendment Reduced the Minimum to Two

Section 1097 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code was amended effective **February 7, 2023**, reducing the minimum number of company promoters from three to two.[^292]

The amendment eliminated the previous three-person requirement. However, it did not authorize ordinary single-shareholder limited companies.

Under Section 1237, a company whose shareholders are reduced to one may face court-ordered dissolution.

**Thai Visa Centre simultaneously overstates the minimum number of shareholders and advertises a one-person company structure that the current law does not generally permit.**

### The Error Appears in Multiple Guides

TVC's separate *Company Registration in Thailand* guide also claims that single-shareholder companies are possible in certain circumstances.[^293]

Neither guide identifies an enacted legal provision establishing the supposed exception.

Foreign ownership approval under the Foreign Business Act, BOI promotion, or treaty arrangements must be distinguished from the minimum number of shareholders required to incorporate an ordinary Thai private limited company.

### Consequences for Entrepreneurs

Foreign entrepreneurs relying on TVC's guidance could unnecessarily recruit a third shareholder or incorrectly assume that they can establish a limited company without any additional shareholder.

Both mistakes can affect corporate ownership arrangements, registration costs, legal documentation, and subsequent visa or work-permit planning.

**The discrepancy is especially concerning because TVC markets business-visa coordination and referrals to company-registration professionals while publishing incorrect incorporation requirements three years after the law changed.**

The agency should correct both guides, identify the applicable Civil and Commercial Code provisions, and distinguish genuine single-owner business structures from private limited companies requiring multiple shareholders.

## Estate-Planning Guides Invent Mandatory Inheritance Shares

Thai Visa Centre publishes incorrect advice about whether Thai law permits people to disinherit children, parents, and other statutory heirs.

Its guide *Probate and Succession in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, claims that descendants have protected minimum inheritance shares.[^235]

The guide's FAQ states:

> Certain descendants entitled to minimum share even if will says otherwise.

It further advises readers that disinheritance is permitted only partially because of supposed forced-heirship restrictions.

**These claims conflict with Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code, which does not generally impose mandatory reserved inheritance shares.**

### Section 1608 Expressly Permits Disinheritance

Section 1608 of the Civil and Commercial Code B.E. 2468 permits a person to disinherit statutory heirs through:

1. An express declaration in a will.
2. A written declaration deposited with the competent official.

The section also establishes that statutory heirs excluded from a will disposing of the entire estate are deemed disinherited, even without individual exclusion clauses.[^238]

Thailand's Department of Provincial Administration confirms these principles in its 2026 will-writing handbook, citing Supreme Court decisions concerning complete and partial testamentary dispositions.[^239]

This differs fundamentally from forced-heirship systems that reserve fixed portions of an estate for children or other relatives regardless of testamentary instructions.

### The Same Error Appears Across Multiple Guides

TVC's *Thai Probate and Inheritance Law* guide claims that valid wills override intestate succession only partially because of forced-heirship restrictions.[^236]

Its *Foreigner Making a Will in Thailand* guide goes further, stating that Thai law reserves mandatory inheritance shares for spouses, children, and parents.

It explicitly warns that protected heirs cannot be fully disinherited.[^237]

These are not isolated references to ordinary intestate succession. They describe supposed restrictions on legally valid wills.

The latter guide also contradicts itself by separately acknowledging that statutory heir rules apply when a person dies intestate.

### Marital Property Is Not Forced Heirship

There are important qualifications that TVC's guidance fails to distinguish.

Under Section 1625, a surviving spouse's ownership interest in marital property must be determined before the deceased spouse's estate is distributed.

Sections 1620 and 1629–1635 establish statutory inheritance rights where there is no effective will, or where assets remain undisposed of by a will.[^240]

Those provisions do not establish a universal reserved inheritance portion that prevents a valid will from excluding particular relatives.

Wills must still satisfy legal formalities, and questions involving marital property, creditors, invalid testamentary provisions, or cross-border succession may require separate analysis.

**These genuine legal restrictions should not be confused with nonexistent mandatory inheritance shares for every spouse, child, or parent.**

### Consequences for Customers

TVC promotes estate-planning coordination, probate assistance referrals, and property-related legal services to foreigners holding Thai assets.

Its incorrect guidance could discourage people from making lawful testamentary arrangements or cause families to misunderstand their actual inheritance rights.

The consequences could involve significant assets, including condominiums, bank accounts, business interests, and marital property.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC drafted invalid wills, caused inheritance disputes, or obtained payments through these incorrect statements.

Nevertheless, the company repeatedly describes a nonexistent general forced-heirship restriction as an established feature of Thai succession law.

TVC should correct its probate and will-writing guides, distinguish intestate inheritance from testamentary freedom, and accurately explain the separate treatment of marital property and legally ineffective wills.

## Probate Guides Overstate Thai Co-Executor Requirements and Omit Direct Inheritance Transfers

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading estate-planning guidance concerning the appointment of foreign estate administrators and the procedures available for registering inherited property in Thailand.

Its *Probate and Succession in Thailand: Estate Planning for Foreign Residents* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, instructs readers to appoint a Thai co-executor when naming a foreign resident to administer an estate.[^391]

The same guide identifies the appointment of a foreign-only executor without a Thai co-executor for Land Office transfers as a common estate-planning mistake.

Its separate *Probate and Estate Administration in Thailand* guide similarly states that foreign executors often need a Thai co-executor.[^392]

Both publications strongly emphasize court-supervised estate administration and the use of court orders when transferring inherited property.

**These instructions fail to distinguish practical assistance from legal requirements and overlook official procedures permitting qualifying inheritance transfers without a court-appointed estate administrator.**

### Thai Law Does Not Generally Require Estate Administrators to Be Thai Nationals

Sections 1711–1718 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code govern the appointment and eligibility of estate administrators.

Section 1715 expressly permits a testator to appoint one or more people to administer the estate.

Section 1718 identifies the persons legally disqualified from serving as administrators.[^393]

The disqualifications concern:

1. Persons who have not attained the required legal capacity.
2. Persons of unsound mind or declared quasi-incompetent.
3. Persons adjudged bankrupt.

**Foreign nationality is not a statutory disqualification.**

The Code also does not establish a general requirement that every foreign administrator serve alongside a Thai national.

A Thai co-administrator may be useful where an estate includes substantial local assets, complicated administrative procedures, or beneficiaries living in multiple countries.

However, practical convenience does not make joint appointment legally mandatory.

TVC's warnings do not adequately explain that distinction.

### Supreme Court Decision 626/2541 Recognizes Foreign Administrators

Thailand's Supreme Court has directly addressed whether foreign nationality prevents a person from administering an estate.

In Decision 626/2541, a foreign woman sought appointment as administrator of her deceased husband's estate.

The deceased's child opposed the petition.

The Supreme Court held that foreign nationality did not disqualify the petitioner under Section 1718 of the Civil and Commercial Code.[^394]

The court considered her suitability for administration and appointed her jointly with the deceased's child.

The decision establishes two important principles.

First, **a foreign national can lawfully qualify to administer a Thai estate**.

Second, the court retains discretion to determine whether a particular person is suitable and whether a joint appointment is appropriate.

The fact that the court appointed joint administrators in that particular case does not create a universal requirement for foreign administrators to have Thai co-executors.

Subsequent judicial guidance confirms that courts assess suitability, good faith, competing interests, and the circumstances of the estate.

Nationality alone does not determine the outcome.

**TVC's advice presents Thai co-administration as a routine necessity without identifying the statutory rules or the Supreme Court decision recognizing foreign administrators.**

### Appointing Joint Administrators Can Create Additional Complications

Naming a local co-administrator may be sensible in some circumstances.

A person based in Thailand may be better positioned to deal with banks, the Land Department, courts, translation requirements, and local beneficiaries.

Nevertheless, appointing multiple administrators introduces its own legal considerations.

Section 1726 of the Civil and Commercial Code provides rules governing how joint administrators exercise their authority.[^393]

Unless the will provides otherwise, decisions among multiple administrators generally require majority agreement.

Disagreements between two administrators may consequently require intervention by the court.

A testator should therefore consider:

- Whether a single administrator can perform the necessary duties.
- Whether the proposed administrator is eligible and capable of acting.
- Whether local assistance can be arranged without appointing another administrator.
- Whether joint appointment would create divided authority or additional administrative costs.
- Whether the will should expressly establish decision-making arrangements.

The recommendation to appoint a Thai co-administrator should be presented as a possible estate-planning choice, not an unavoidable condition imposed on foreigners.

### The Land Department Allows Inheritance Transfers Without an Estate Administrator

TVC's probate publications also leave out an important alternative to court-supervised administration.

Section 81 of Thailand's Land Code establishes a procedure through which qualifying heirs may apply to register inherited rights directly with the competent Land Office.[^395]

Under that procedure, the applicant submits evidence establishing entitlement to the inherited property.

The official reviews the documentation and ordinarily publishes an inheritance notice.

The statutory notice period is generally 30 days.

If no qualifying objection is raised and the officer is satisfied that the applicant is entitled to the inheritance, registration may proceed according to the applicable requirements.

Where objections arise, the officer may investigate the competing claims.

Unresolved disputes can require court proceedings.

**The existence of this procedure means that obtaining a court order appointing an administrator is not universally required before an inheritance can be registered.**

The Department of Lands explicitly publishes a public service procedure titled *Registration of Inheritance Transfer — Without an Estate Administrator*.[^396]

Its instructions address inherited land, buildings, and condominium ownership documents.

The procedure includes:

1. Submission of the deceased person's property documents.
2. Evidence of death and the applicant's legal entitlement.
3. Examination of the applicant's inheritance rights.
4. Publication of the required notice.
5. Consideration of any objections.
6. Registration of the inheritance if the applicable conditions are satisfied.

The Department of Lands has also published explanatory guidance distinguishing inheritance transfers with an administrator from transfers without one.[^397]

It identifies the latter as an established procedure involving additional verification and notification requirements.

**TVC's court-focused presentation overlooks this officially recognized alternative.**

### Court-Appointed Administrators Remain Necessary in Many Cases

The existence of direct inheritance registration does not mean that heirs can always avoid court proceedings.

Court appointment can be required or practically necessary where:

- The heirs disagree about entitlement or distribution.
- An existing administrator cannot perform the required duties.
- A will contains disputed or ineffective provisions.
- Banks or other institutions require judicial confirmation of authority.
- Assets must be sold or administered before distribution.
- The Land Office cannot resolve competing claims through its administrative procedure.
- Complex cross-border succession issues prevent straightforward registration.

Section 1713 of the Civil and Commercial Code identifies the circumstances in which an heir, interested person, or public prosecutor may petition for the appointment of an administrator.[^393]

Foreign families may also encounter additional documentation and authentication requirements.

A foreign will, overseas death certificate, or foreign-issued proof of relationship may need translation and legalization before the relevant Thai institution accepts it.

Accordingly, TVC is not necessarily wrong when it states that many inheritance matters involve court orders.

**The problem is its failure to explain that court appointment is not invariably required and that the correct procedure depends on the assets, beneficiaries, and circumstances of the estate.**

### Administrative Authority Is Different From Beneficial Ownership

TVC's discussion also risks confusing the nationality of the estate administrator with the nationality of the heir.

An administrator manages and distributes assets on behalf of the estate.

An heir acquires rights under the applicable succession provisions.

These are distinct legal roles.

A foreigner may lawfully serve as estate administrator without necessarily being permitted to acquire every asset personally.

For example, the Land Code imposes separate restrictions on foreign ownership of land.

The Condominium Act contains different rules governing foreign ownership and inherited condominium units.

A foreign administrator's eligibility to perform administrative duties therefore does not establish personal entitlement to inherited land.

Conversely, restrictions on a foreigner's right to own particular property do not automatically prevent that person from administering the estate.

**The correct legal analysis separates the administrator's eligibility, the beneficiaries' inheritance rights, and the registration requirements for each asset.**

### Financial Consequences for Foreign Families

These distinctions matter for foreign retirees, spouses, and families holding Thai property, bank deposits, company shares, and other investments.

A family relying on TVC's guidance might incorrectly believe that it must:

- Appoint an additional Thai co-executor.
- Replace a foreign executor already designated in a valid will.
- Commence court proceedings before investigating direct Land Office registration.
- Retain additional professional representatives for an otherwise straightforward inheritance.
- Delay distribution while seeking a court order that the relevant government agency may not require.

In complex estates, these steps may be appropriate.

In simpler circumstances, they may create unnecessary expense, delays, and divided administrative authority.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has charged customers for unnecessary probate proceedings, improperly arranged estate administrators, or caused financial losses through these publications.

Nevertheless, **its published instructions can create an exaggerated impression of the legal barriers facing foreigners administering Thai estates.**

### Recommended Corrections

Thai Visa Centre should revise its probate and succession publications to explain:

- Sections 1711–1718 of the Civil and Commercial Code and the statutory eligibility rules for administrators.
- Supreme Court Decision 626/2541 confirming that foreign nationality is not an automatic disqualification.
- The distinction between optional Thai co-administration and a legally mandatory appointment.
- The circumstances in which court appointment is appropriate under Section 1713.
- Direct inheritance registration under Section 81 of the Land Code.
- The Department of Lands' procedure for transfers without a court-appointed administrator.
- The separate rules governing foreign administrators, foreign heirs, and foreign ownership of immovable property.

**The agency should avoid portraying Thai co-executors and court-appointed probate administration as universal requirements when Thailand's legislation and published Land Department procedures establish more flexible alternatives.**

## Remarriage FAQ Denies Statutory Waiting Period and Uses Obsolete Marriage Age

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *Divorcee Getting Married in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly tells foreign customers that Thai law imposes no waiting period after divorce.[^241]

The page repeatedly makes categorical claims, including:

> No statutory waiting period for remarriage once divorce is final.

It also describes marriage as available from age 17 with parental consent.

**Both statements conflict with Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code as amended by the Marriage Equality Act, effective January 23, 2025.**

### Section 1453 Retains a 310-Day Remarriage Restriction

Section 1453 establishes a 310-day waiting period for women remarrying men following the termination of a previous marriage, subject to four statutory exceptions:[^242]

1. The woman has given birth during the waiting period.
2. She is remarrying her former spouse.
3. A qualified medical practitioner certifies that she is not pregnant.
4. A court authorizes the marriage.

The provision does not impose an equivalent waiting period on divorced men. Its amended wording also distinguishes women remarrying men from other marriage configurations.

Thailand's Government Public Relations Department confirmed the continuing 310-day restriction when explaining the January 2025 Marriage Equality Act.[^243]

**TVC's assurance that no statutory waiting period exists is therefore incorrect for applicants covered by Section 1453.**

### TVC's Own Separate FAQ Explains the Rule Correctly

The contradiction is particularly clear because TVC publishes another FAQ titled *Waiting Period After Divorce - Remarriage in Thailand*.[^244]

That page correctly explains the 310-day restriction, the non-pregnancy certificate exception, and the different treatment of male divorcees.

It also warns that district offices can refuse premature marriage-registration applications.

Both FAQs claim to reflect June 2026 requirements, yet they provide opposite answers to the same legal question.

The incorrect FAQ further provides a suggested 10–14-day remarriage timeline without incorporating the applicable waiting-period requirements.

### Marriage Age Was Raised to 18 in January 2025

The incorrect FAQ also describes the ordinary marriage-age threshold as 17 with parental consent.[^241]

TVC's separate *Marriage in Thailand* guide repeats the outdated minimum age of 17.[^245]

However, the 2024 amendment to Section 1448, effective January 23, 2025, raised the normal minimum marriage age to 18 for both parties.[^242]

Marriage below 18 requires court permission based on appropriate grounds. Parental consent alone does not replace that judicial authorization.

Separate consent requirements may still apply to persons aged 18 or 19.

**TVC therefore continues publishing pre-2025 marriage-age guidance in materials claiming to explain current registration requirements.**

### Consequences for Customers

These discrepancies matter because TVC advertises marriage-document preparation, embassy coordination, district-office registration, and immigration assistance.

Customers relying on the incorrect guidance could arrange travel, embassy appointments, translations, and registration dates without satisfying the applicable marriage requirements.

A refused registration could also disrupt plans to apply for marriage-based immigration status.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has caused failed registrations or financial losses through this guidance.

Nevertheless, the company presents contradictory legal instructions while advertising professional marriage-registration assistance.

TVC should correct its remarriage FAQ, identify the scope and exceptions of Section 1453, update its minimum-age guidance, and reconcile its published marriage-registration instructions.

## Family-Law Guide Misstates Prenuptial Registration Timing

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Family Law Questions – Marriage, Divorce, and Visas* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, advises couples that a prenuptial agreement must be registered at the same district office as their marriage and completed before the wedding day.[^331]

The guidance repeats this requirement in its family-property overview.
