- Publish notice of the company's dissolution in the prescribed manner.
- Notify known creditors through registered correspondence.
- Inform creditors that claims should be submitted to the liquidator for payment.

The DBD's published instructions confirm that these creditor-notification steps must be completed within **14 days of dissolution**.[^379]

These notices matter because liquidation is not simply the process of closing bank accounts and distributing assets to shareholders.

The liquidator must identify liabilities, collect receivables, settle creditor claims, and preserve funds required to discharge the company's obligations.

TVC warns against distributing assets before the creditor-notification period has been completed.

However, its published checklist does not clearly explain when the notification obligation begins or how it relates to the company's dissolution date.

**A guide claiming to describe company closure in strict chronological order should identify these mandatory deadlines at the start of the process, rather than focusing almost exclusively on the final registration deadline.**

### The Later Shareholders' Meeting Serves a Different Purpose

The existence of a later shareholders' meeting requires careful distinction.

Sections 1255 and 1256 of the Civil and Commercial Code require liquidators to prepare an opening liquidation balance sheet, arrange an audit, and convene a general meeting.[^378]

That meeting serves specific purposes:

- Adopting the audited balance sheet.
- Confirming the directors as liquidators or appointing replacements.
- Considering additional directions relating to the winding-up of the company.

It is therefore possible for a company to have a shareholders' meeting after the original dissolution resolution.

**But this meeting is not a second vote legally required to confirm the original dissolution resolution before DBD registration.**

The Department of Business Development documents the liquidation meeting separately from the procedure for registering the company's dissolution.[^380]

This distinction reconciles the statutory provisions without incorrectly suggesting that all subsequent shareholder meetings are unnecessary.

A company may also need annual meetings while liquidation remains unfinished and a final meeting to approve the completed liquidation accounts.

Those meetings have different statutory purposes and should not be combined into one generic confirmation requirement.

### Dissolution and Completion of Liquidation Are Different Legal Events

TVC's guide states that a company is not fully dissolved after the first shareholders' resolution.

It later claims that the company becomes legally dissolved once the final liquidation registration is completed.

This wording conflates dissolution with the termination of the company's remaining legal existence.

Section 1236 establishes the events that cause a company to be dissolved, including a valid special resolution.[^377]

Section 1249 separately provides that a dissolved company is deemed to continue in existence insofar as necessary for liquidation.[^378]

Consequently:

1. **Dissolution:** The company enters the winding-up process following the legally effective dissolution event.
2. **Liquidation:** Its legal existence continues for the limited purposes of settling debts, realizing assets, and completing statutory obligations.
3. **Completion:** The final liquidation accounts are approved and completion is registered, ending the winding-up process.

Section 1270 governs the final meeting and registration of completed liquidation.[^378]

The company therefore does not remain an ordinary operating company until the final registration.

Nor does its legal personality necessarily disappear immediately upon dissolution.

**The distinction is between a dissolved company continuing for liquidation and a company whose liquidation has been completed.**

TVC should use those terms consistently rather than suggesting that the special resolution has no legal dissolving effect until the entire liquidation is finished.

### Potential Financial and Compliance Consequences

The errors could matter for foreign entrepreneurs, directors, shareholders, and creditors involved in closing Thai businesses.

A company owner relying on TVC's instructions might:

- Schedule an unnecessary confirmation meeting before filing with the DBD.
- Miss the 14-day dissolution-registration deadline.
- Delay the statutory publication and creditor-notification requirements.
- Misunderstand when directors become liquidators and which powers they retain.
- Confuse initial dissolution registration with registration of completed liquidation.
- Make incorrect assumptions about ongoing contracts, employee obligations, or corporate authority.
- Incur avoidable accounting, legal, or administrative expenses.

These risks are particularly relevant where foreign employees and directors also hold Non-Immigrant B visas or work permits linked to the business.

Company dissolution, termination of employment, and immigration permissions do not necessarily take effect at identical times.

Each requires separate review under the applicable rules.

### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination and work-permit assistance for foreign directors and employees whose companies are winding down.

Its dissolution guide encourages business owners to contact the agency before cancelling employment-linked immigration permissions.

The company does not claim to act as the official company registrar.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has caused late DBD filings, failed to notify creditors, improperly arranged shareholder meetings, or charged customers for unnecessary corporate procedures.

Nevertheless, its published instructions create confusion about a process governed by specific statutory requirements and deadlines.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct the dissolution guide to distinguish the single special resolution under Section 1194, initial DBD registration under Section 1254, creditor notifications under Section 1253, subsequent audited-balance-sheet approval under Sections 1255–1256, and completion of liquidation under Section 1270.**

The revised guide should explain that dissolution and final liquidation registration are separate legal milestones, and that an unnecessary confirmation meeting must not delay mandatory filings.

## Bankruptcy Guide Conflates Liquidation, Rehabilitation, and Automatic Stay Protections

Thai Visa Centre's *Bankruptcy: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents Thailand's bankruptcy and business rehabilitation procedures as parts of a generalized insolvency process.[^354]

Its procedural overview states that either a creditor or debtor files a petition with the Central Bankruptcy Court, followed by an absolute receivership order, creditor meetings, and either reorganization or liquidation.

The guide also prominently claims that filing triggers an automatic stay on most creditor enforcement while an official receiver or rehabilitation planner administers the estate.

**These explanations fail to distinguish ordinary bankruptcy liquidation from business rehabilitation, which have different filing rights, legal requirements, and consequences for creditors.**

### Ordinary Bankruptcy Is Generally Initiated by Creditors

Section 9 of Thailand's Bankruptcy Act B.E. 2483 (1940) establishes the ordinary bankruptcy petition procedure.[^355]

Under the provision, a creditor may seek bankruptcy proceedings where:

- The debtor is insolvent.
- An individual debtor owes at least 1 million baht to one or more petitioning creditors.
- A juristic-person debtor owes at least 2 million baht to one or more petitioning creditors.
- The relevant debt is definite and legally determinable.

The court must examine the statutory conditions before ordering absolute receivership under Section 14.

**An insolvent individual cannot ordinarily initiate voluntary personal bankruptcy merely by submitting a petition to obtain protection from creditors.**

Chambers and Partners confirms that ordinary Thai bankruptcy proceedings are creditor-initiated rather than a voluntary bankruptcy procedure available to debtors.[^357]

A separate statutory exception exists where the liquidator of a company undergoing winding-up applies for bankruptcy after discovering that its liabilities exceed its assets.

That exception should not be confused with a general right for individuals or company directors to initiate ordinary bankruptcy proceedings.

TVC's generalized petition-filing sequence fails to explain these distinctions.

### Business Rehabilitation Uses a Separate Statutory Procedure

Sections 90/2, 90/3, and 90/4 of the Bankruptcy Act establish a separate procedure for business rehabilitation.[^356]

Unlike ordinary bankruptcy liquidation, qualifying rehabilitation proceedings may be initiated by:

1. The debtor.
2. One or more qualifying creditors.
3. Authorized government supervisory authorities in specified circumstances.

Under the ordinary corporate rehabilitation framework, Section 90/3 provides for a minimum indebtedness of 10 million baht and requires reasonable grounds and prospects for successfully rehabilitating the business.

Separate rules may apply to qualifying small and medium-sized enterprises.

The objective of rehabilitation is to preserve a viable business while restructuring its financial obligations under court supervision.

Ordinary bankruptcy instead focuses on receivership, realizing the debtor's assets, and distributing proceeds among creditors.

KPMG's May 2026 analysis independently identifies the debtor, creditors, and relevant government authorities as potential rehabilitation petitioners, subject to the statutory conditions.[^358]

**A rehabilitation petition filed by a company is not equivalent to an ordinary bankruptcy petition filed against that company.**

TVC's guide acknowledges that rehabilitation can preserve a business, but its combined procedural explanation does not adequately distinguish the separate statutory pathways.

### Automatic Stay Does Not Arise From Every Bankruptcy Filing

The discrepancy is particularly significant in TVC's description of creditor enforcement.

The guide's summary prominently states that filing triggers a stay on most collection activity.

However, Section 90/12 of the Bankruptcy Act specifically establishes automatic-stay protections in business rehabilitation proceedings.[^356]

The protections arise when the court issues an order accepting the rehabilitation petition for consideration, subject to the statutory limitations and exceptions.

They can restrict:

- New civil litigation against the debtor concerning qualifying obligations.
- Continuation of certain pending proceedings.
- Enforcement against the debtor's property.
- Enforcement of specified security interests without the necessary authorization.
- Certain transfers, disposals, and payments by the debtor.

Thailand's Legal Execution Department independently illustrates this sequence in its official business rehabilitation flowchart.[^359]

**The legally significant event is the court's acceptance of the rehabilitation petition, not merely a debtor or creditor preparing or submitting paperwork.**

Ordinary bankruptcy proceedings operate differently.

The filing of a creditor's bankruptcy petition does not automatically create the broad rehabilitation moratorium under Section 90/12.

The court may impose specific provisional measures where statutory conditions are met. An absolute receivership order subsequently transfers control of the debtor's assets to the official receiver under Section 22.

These measures are legally distinct from the automatic stay applicable during business rehabilitation.

TVC's generalized descriptions risk obscuring that distinction.

### Receivership and Rehabilitation Planning Are Different Stages

The guide's procedural sequence also places absolute receivership before discussions of either rehabilitation or asset distribution.

This presentation conflates the administration of a bankrupt estate with the appointment and functions of a rehabilitation plan preparer.

In ordinary bankruptcy, the official receiver assumes statutory authority over the debtor's assets following the relevant court order.

Business rehabilitation follows a different framework involving the court, official receiver, appointed plan preparer, creditors, and subsequent plan administration.

The court-supervised rehabilitation procedure can allow a qualifying debtor business to continue operating while its debts are restructured.

The existence of a bankruptcy petition does not mean a company automatically proceeds through absolute receivership and then chooses between liquidation and rehabilitation.

**These are different statutory processes, not interchangeable outcomes of a single filing sequence.**

### Financial Consequences for Foreign Creditors and Business Owners

The distinction matters for foreign entrepreneurs, investors, directors, lenders, and employees involved with financially distressed Thai businesses.

A company owner relying on TVC's guidance could mistakenly believe that submitting an ordinary bankruptcy petition would immediately prevent creditors from taking enforcement action.

A creditor might similarly misunderstand whether a debtor has obtained statutory protection, whether a separate claim can proceed, or when enforcement restrictions take effect.

Potential consequences include:

- Incorrect assumptions about whether a company qualifies for rehabilitation.
- Failure to distinguish voluntary corporate rehabilitation from creditor-initiated bankruptcy.
- Unnecessary litigation or procedural expenses.
- Misunderstanding the enforceability of security interests.
- Mistaken decisions concerning asset transfers, debt repayment, or ongoing operations.
- Delays securing legal advice before insolvency proceedings affect visas and work permits.

These are substantial financial and legal risks, particularly where company assets, employee wages, personal guarantees, or immigration sponsorship depend on the outcome.

### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination and referrals to licensed Thai insolvency lawyers for foreign business owners affected by bankruptcy or company restructuring.

The company expressly states that it is not a law firm and does not represent customers in bankruptcy proceedings.

That distinction limits what can reasonably be inferred about its involvement in individual cases.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has filed improper bankruptcy petitions, advised customers to disregard creditor enforcement, or caused financial losses through these publications.

Nevertheless, **its guide presents legally distinct insolvency procedures through an oversimplified sequence while directing foreign business owners toward professional assistance.**

TVC should revise the guide to distinguish ordinary creditor-initiated bankruptcy, qualifying corporate rehabilitation, statutory liquidator applications, and the separate conditions under which automatic-stay or receivership protections take effect.

It should also identify the applicable filing thresholds and statutory provisions rather than implying that all forms of insolvency follow the same petition and enforcement procedure.

## Class-Action Guide Misstates Statutory Framework and Opt-Out Rights

Thai Visa Centre's *Class Action Lawsuits: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly presents Thai class-action litigation as a collection of separate statutory procedures with different rules governing participation.[^360]

Its introductory explanation states that Thailand permits grouped claims under specific legislation, including consumer protection and securities laws, rather than through a general class-action framework.

The publication also claims that opt-in and opt-out requirements vary depending on the governing statute.

Its checklist warns potential claimants about missing opt-in registration deadlines, while its frequently asked questions similarly suggest that participation may require individuals to register before becoming class members.

**These explanations misrepresent Thailand's established civil-procedure framework and confuse automatic class membership with the separate process for claiming compensation after judgment.**

### Thailand Established a General Class-Action Framework in 2015

Thailand introduced formal class-action proceedings through the Civil Procedure Code Amendment (No. 26) B.E. 2558 (2015).

The amendments inserted Sections 222/1 through 222/49, establishing a general mechanism for representative civil litigation.[^361]

Thailand's Securities and Exchange Commission explains that the initiative originally arose from concerns about securities investors' access to compensation.

However, the Office of the Council of State expanded the framework beyond securities disputes to accommodate multiple claimants suffering harm from torts, contractual breaches, and violations of other laws.[^361]

The resulting mechanism is part of Thailand's Civil Procedure Code rather than a collection of unrelated opt-in schemes.[^366]

The framework can apply to qualifying disputes involving:

- Contractual breaches.
- Wrongful acts and tortious conduct.
- Consumer protection.
- Securities and financial markets.
- Environmental damage.
- Labour rights.
- Trade competition.

The Legal 500's comparative guide independently confirms the broad statutory framework and identifies Section 222/8 as governing the categories of claims potentially suitable for collective proceedings.[^362]

Specialized statutes and procedural rules may affect jurisdiction, remedies, and the handling of individual disputes.

Nevertheless, **the existence of specialized legislation does not eliminate the general class-action provisions of the Civil Procedure Code.**

TVC's guide fails to explain this distinction.

### Class Membership Is Generally Automatic, Not Opt-In

The more consequential discrepancy concerns participation rights.

Thailand's Civil Procedure Code establishes an **opt-out** class-action system.

Once the court certifies a class, qualifying persons falling within its definition are generally included without having to submit individual applications to join.

Section 222/15 establishes notice requirements following certification.[^363]

These include notification of known class members, public notice, and an explanation of the period during which affected persons may elect to leave the class.

Section 222/16 grants class members the right to opt out by submitting written notice to the court within the prescribed period.[^364]

The ordinary opt-out period cannot be shorter than 45 days.

Class members who do not exercise that right generally remain bound by the resulting judgment.

Thailand's Securities and Exchange Commission explicitly confirms that qualifying affected persons become class members by operation of law and must notify the court if they wish to exclude themselves.[^361]

**This directly contradicts TVC's repeated implication that prospective class members must determine whether their particular statute requires them to opt in before participating.**

The distinction is fundamental.

Failure to join an opt-in proceeding ordinarily means that a person remains outside the participating group.

Failure to opt out of Thailand's general class-action procedure can instead mean that the person remains included and bound by the court's decision.

Those outcomes have materially different legal consequences.

### Compensation Claims Are Not Applications to Join the Class

TVC's guidance also contains a more subtle problem.

It repeatedly warns that missing registration deadlines may prevent individuals from receiving compensation.

Deadlines for claiming compensation can indeed be important.

However, **the requirement to submit a compensation claim following judgment is not the same as a requirement to opt in to the lawsuit.**

Section 222/39 of the Civil Procedure Code governs the enforcement of qualifying class-action judgments involving monetary compensation.[^365]

Following the judgment, the court notifies class members and appoints an execution officer for the relevant payment procedure.

The court establishes a period during which class members must submit requests for payment.

A member who fails to submit the required request within that period may lose entitlement to participate in the distribution, subject to the statutory exceptions.

This creates two legally distinct stages:

1. **Class membership:** Qualifying persons are included following certification unless they exercise their statutory right to opt out.
2. **Payment claims:** Following a favorable monetary judgment, members must comply with the applicable compensation-claim procedure to receive their individual shares.

A person can therefore remain bound by the class-action judgment while losing the opportunity to receive money through the subsequent distribution process.

TVC's guide fails to distinguish these stages and instead repeatedly characterizes participation deadlines as opt-in requirements.

### Court Certification Remains Mandatory

The existence of a general statutory framework does not mean that every group of people sharing similar grievances can automatically pursue a class action.

Section 222/12 establishes requirements that courts must consider when determining whether class proceedings are appropriate.

Relevant considerations include common factual and legal issues, the suitability of the representative plaintiff, the adequacy of legal counsel, and whether collective litigation is more appropriate than separate actions.[^362]

The courts retain authority to refuse class certification.

A disappointed claimant cannot simply declare a dispute to be a class action, nor does having multiple complainants eliminate ordinary evidentiary and jurisdictional requirements.

TVC correctly recognizes that judicial certification is necessary.

The problem is its failure to connect that requirement with the broader Civil Procedure Code framework and the statutory consequences of certification.

### Financial and Procedural Consequences

These errors could affect foreign consumers, investors, property buyers, and business owners participating in litigation involving multiple claimants.

A person relying on TVC's instructions might:

- Incorrectly believe that failing to register initially means they are not part of a class action.
- Overlook a critical opportunity to opt out and preserve an individual legal claim.
- Confuse a court's certification notice with a later compensation-payment notice.
- Misunderstand whether an unfavorable class judgment will bind them.
- Assume that collective proceedings are unavailable for ordinary contractual or tortious claims.
- Miss the separate deadline for claiming their share of a monetary judgment.

The potential consequences are not limited to administrative inconvenience.

They can involve the loss of individual litigation rights, missed financial recovery, and misunderstanding the binding effect of court judgments.

The general class-action framework applies regardless of whether the claimant is Thai or foreign, although jurisdiction, standing, and the substantive cause of action must still satisfy Thai law.

### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination, document preparation, and referrals to Thai litigation lawyers for foreigners involved in collective disputes.

Its class-action guide specifically targets investors, consumers, and foreign residents who may require professional assistance navigating the litigation process.

The company states that it is not a law firm and does not itself represent customers in class-action proceedings.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has improperly enrolled customers in litigation, caused claimants to miss statutory deadlines, or collected fees for unnecessary proceedings.

Nevertheless, its published instructions repeatedly confuse the procedural rights that determine whether affected persons are legally bound by a class-action judgment.

**TVC should revise its guide to identify Sections 222/1–222/49 of the Civil Procedure Code, explain automatic class membership and the right to opt out, distinguish certification notices from post-judgment compensation claims, and clarify the separate deadlines governing each stage.**

## Trade Disputes Guide Names a Nonexistent Commercial Court and Misstates Customs Appeal Procedures

Thai Visa Centre's *Trade Disputes in Thailand: Import, Export, and Customs Litigation*, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly identifies a supposed "Bangkok Commercial Court" as the appropriate forum for commercial trade litigation.[^367]

The guide directs foreign importers and exporters toward this court for claims involving letters of credit, contract enforcement, international shipments, and distribution agreements.

Its procedural checklist specifically instructs readers to file complaints at the Bangkok Commercial Court.

The same publication repeatedly directs customs classification and valuation disputes toward administrative-court proceedings, without explaining the statutory Customs Appeal Committee process.

**These instructions misidentify Thailand's judicial institutions and omit critical procedures governing challenges to customs duty assessments.**

### Thailand Has No Court Officially Named the Bangkok Commercial Court

Thailand's Office of the Judiciary publishes the structure of its national court system.[^368]

The recognized courts include ordinary Courts of First Instance and specialized courts established to handle particular categories of disputes.

There is no separate institution officially named the "Bangkok Commercial Court."

The actual courts relevant to the types of cases TVC describes include:

- **Ordinary civil courts:** Certain domestic contractual and commercial disputes.
- **Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court:** Qualifying international trade transactions and associated disputes.
- **Central Tax Court:** Tax and customs matters falling within its statutory jurisdiction.
- **Administrative Court:** Qualifying public-law disputes within its separate jurisdiction.

The judiciary's published descriptions identify the Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court and Central Tax Court as specialized courts with distinct responsibilities.[^369]

Simply describing a dispute as commercial does not establish which court has jurisdiction.

Instead, the applicable law, nature of the transaction, parties, requested remedy, and jurisdictional rules must be examined.

**TVC repeatedly uses the name of a court that does not exist as a formally established Thai judicial institution, without identifying the actual forum in which the relevant claim should be filed.**

### International Trade and Letter-of-Credit Cases Have Specialized Jurisdiction

The discrepancy is especially clear for international letters of credit.

Section 7 of the Act on the Establishment of and Procedure for the Intellectual Property and International Trade Court B.E. 2539 (1996) defines the specialized court's jurisdiction.[^370]

The provision includes qualifying civil disputes concerning:

- International sales and exchanges of goods.
- International services and carriage.
- Related insurance and commercial transactions.
- Letters of credit connected with international trade.
- Associated remittances, trust receipts, and guarantees.

These matters may therefore fall within the jurisdiction of the Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court, rather than the unspecified commercial court described by TVC.

Published analysis of Thai letter-of-credit litigation confirms that qualifying disputes under Section 7(6) belong before the specialized international trade court.[^371]

However, this does not mean every unpaid invoice, domestic distribution agreement, or business-contract dispute automatically belongs there.

Ordinary commercial disputes outside the specialized court's jurisdiction may instead be heard by the appropriate civil court.

**The correct legal distinction is between the jurisdiction of established courts, not between a generic Bangkok Commercial Court and all other forums.**

### Customs Duty Assessments Follow a Statutory Appeal Process

The more consequential problem concerns TVC's explanation of customs disputes.

Its guide identifies customs valuation and tariff-classification challenges as matters requiring administrative-court review.

However, the Customs Act B.E. 2560 (2017) establishes a specific appeal procedure for importers and exporters disputing customs duty assessments.[^372]

Under Section 37, the importer or exporter generally has **30 days from receipt of the assessment notification** to appeal to the Customs Appeal Committee.

This is a formal statutory procedure.

It is not simply an informal negotiation with Customs officials, nor should it be confused with filing a new administrative-court lawsuit.

The Customs Appeal Committee reviews the assessment and issues its ruling under the applicable statutory provisions.

Section 48 provides a further right to commence court proceedings within **30 days after receiving the Appeal Committee's ruling**, subject to the statutory requirements and exceptions.

Section 42 separately addresses the ability to commence proceedings when the committee does not complete its review within the applicable statutory period.

These provisions establish distinct administrative and judicial stages.

**TVC's guide does not explain the Section 37 appeal requirement or the separate time limit for challenging an adverse Appeal Committee ruling.**

### Customs Appeals Do Not Automatically Suspend Payment

The omission extends to the financial consequences of disputing an assessment.

Section 38 of the Customs Act establishes that appealing a duty assessment does not automatically defer payment.[^372]

An importer or exporter generally remains subject to the assessment unless the Director-General permits payment to be deferred pending the relevant appellate decision or final judgment.

This distinction can be financially significant.

A business facing a substantial disputed assessment cannot safely assume that filing an objection, appointing a lawyer, or commencing proceedings automatically prevents customs enforcement.

Additional statutory rules govern unpaid duty, surcharges, penalties, detention of goods, and enforcement measures.

An importer must therefore consider payment obligations and any available deferral procedure separately from the merits of its appeal.

**TVC emphasizes that customs litigation can affect substantial shipments and business operations but fails to explain this essential distinction between appealing an assessment and obtaining relief from immediate payment.**

### The Central Tax Court Handles Civil Customs Duty Litigation

Thailand's Central Tax Court has statutory jurisdiction over qualifying tax disputes, including civil customs duty matters.

The judiciary identifies the court's responsibilities as including appeals involving decisions by competent tax officials or committees and disputes over tax obligations and collection.[^369]

Professional guidance on Thai tax litigation likewise identifies customs duty-assessment disputes among the matters falling within the tax-court framework.[^373]

Consequently, a typical dispute over an additional customs duty assessment does not automatically proceed to the Administrative Court merely because Customs is a government agency.

An importer contesting the assessment must consider the particular statutory appeal procedure and the jurisdiction of the relevant court.

That does not mean the Administrative Court can never hear a customs-related dispute.

Certain disputes involving different administrative acts, regulatory decisions, or public-law questions may fall under separate jurisdictional rules.

Likewise, customs criminal proceedings can involve additional statutory provisions and jurisdictional requirements.

However, **TVC's broad advice omits the central distinction between appealing a customs duty assessment and challenging a different administrative decision.**

### Foreign Importers Face Strict Deadlines and Significant Financial Risks

The combined errors matter because foreign traders frequently operate under short filing deadlines while dealing with substantial financial exposure.

A business relying on TVC's trade-disputes guide could:

- Seek legal representation for proceedings in an incorrectly named court.
- Prepare an administrative-court claim rather than following the required Customs Appeal Committee procedure.
- Miss the initial 30-day duty-assessment appeal period.
- Miss the separate court-filing deadline following an adverse committee ruling.
- Assume that appealing automatically suspends payment obligations.
- Misunderstand whether an international letter-of-credit dispute belongs before a specialized court.
- Incur additional legal expenses correcting procedural errors or jurisdictional misunderstandings.

These risks are particularly relevant to importers facing reassessed customs duties, exporters disputing trade payments, and companies whose ongoing operations depend on timely release of goods.

Failure to comply with a mandatory appeal procedure or statutory filing period can affect the availability of legal remedies.

### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination, translation assistance, and referrals to licensed Thai commercial litigation lawyers.

Its trade-disputes guide specifically targets foreign importers, exporters, company directors, and investors facing customs or commercial disputes.

The company states that it does not provide courtroom representation itself.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has filed proceedings in an incorrect court, caused a customer to miss a Customs Appeal Committee deadline, or generated legal fees through these errors.

Nevertheless, its publications provide procedural instructions that repeatedly name an unrecognized court while overlooking statutory customs appeal requirements.

**TVC should correct the court names in its trade-disputes guide, distinguish ordinary civil litigation from specialized international trade proceedings, explain the Customs Appeal Committee process under Sections 37–48 of the Customs Act, and clarify the separate payment and enforcement consequences of disputing customs duty assessments.**

## Labour Litigation Guide Misstates Court Fees, Lawyer Requirements, and Appeals

Thai Visa Centre's *Labor Litigation: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, contains several misleading statements about Thailand's Labour Court procedures, including the cost of filing claims, the necessity of legal representation, and the court responsible for hearing appeals.[^398]

### Labour Court Proceedings Are Exempt From Court Fees

The guide tells readers that Labour Court fees are "modest compared with Civil Court."

However, Section 27 of the Act on the Establishment of Labour Courts and Labour Court Procedure B.E. 2522 (1979) expressly exempts the filing of claims and proceedings in Labour Courts from court fees.[^399]

Although litigants may incur legal representation, translation, travel, and other expenses, the statutory exemption is substantially different from the guide's suggestion that filing fees are merely reduced.

### Lawyers Are Not Mandatory for Every Labour Court Claim

The guide's frequently asked questions state that Thai counsel is required for proceedings conducted in Thai. Its procedural checklist similarly describes Thai lawyers preparing the necessary pleadings.

Section 35 of the Labour Court legislation expressly allows plaintiffs to submit written claims or present their complaints orally before the court. Where a complaint is presented orally, the court may question the plaintiff and record the claim.[^399]

Legal representation may be advisable, particularly for complex disputes or foreign litigants requiring language assistance, but it is not a universal statutory requirement for bringing a Labour Court claim.

**The distinction matters because incorrect advice about mandatory legal representation could discourage workers from pursuing claims they are legally entitled to file themselves.**

### Appeals Are Directed to the Wrong Court

The guide also instructs readers that appeals proceed to a "Labour Court appellate division."

Thailand instead operates a **Court of Appeal for Specialized Cases**, which includes a Labour Case Division. The court was inaugurated on October 1, 2016, and hears qualifying appeals from the country's specialized courts.[^400]

Thai Visa Centre's description fails to identify the actual appellate institution and omits important procedural restrictions governing Labour Court appeals.

### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Professional Claims

These errors appear in a publication specifically intended to explain legal procedures to foreign residents and workers.

The guide repeatedly recommends professional legal assistance while failing to explain that Labour Court proceedings are exempt from court fees and that individuals may initiate claims without retaining an attorney.

**The combination of incorrect cost information, overstated representation requirements, and misidentified appellate procedures raises serious questions about the accuracy of Thai Visa Centre's professionally presented legal guidance.**

The discrepancies do not establish deliberate deception. However, they undermine confidence in a guide presented as reviewed and suitable for readers navigating potentially consequential employment disputes.


## Consumer Protection Guide Falsely Claims Lawyers Are Mandatory and Omits Free Court Filing

Thai Visa Centre's *Consumer Protection Act: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, misrepresents fundamental consumer rights under Thai law. The guide incorrectly tells foreigners that lawyers are required to pursue consumer litigation, fails to disclose statutory court-fee exemptions, and invents documentary requirements that could discourage legitimate complaints.[^401]

### Falsely Claims Thai Lawyers Are Mandatory

The guide explicitly states:

> You still need Thai counsel to file and conduct proceedings in Thai.

Its procedural checklist repeatedly instructs consumers to use Thai lawyers to prepare and file complaints.

**Section 20 of Thailand's Consumer Case Procedure Act B.E. 2551 (2008) directly contradicts this advice.** Consumers may file complaints orally or in writing. Court officials must record oral complaints and obtain the plaintiff's signature.[^402]

Thai law deliberately provides a filing procedure accessible to consumers without mandatory legal representation.

TVC instead presents paid legal assistance as a prerequisite.

### Conceals Statutory Court-Fee Exemptions

Section 18 of the same legislation exempts qualifying consumer plaintiffs from court fees when filing and conducting consumer proceedings.[^402]

The exemption does not eliminate possible liability for costs at the conclusion of proceedings, but it removes a significant upfront financial barrier.

**TVC's guide omits this fundamental consumer protection entirely**, despite extensively discussing litigation costs, legal representation, and referrals to professional lawyers.

### Invents Mandatory Receipt and Warranty Requirements

The guide further claims that consumer complaints involving defective electronics fail without Thai-language receipts and warranty cards.

Neither Section 20's complaint-filing procedure nor the legislation's general consumer-case provisions impose these documents as universal filing requirements.[^402]

Receipts and warranty cards may provide useful evidence, but their absence does not automatically invalidate a consumer complaint.

TVC converts recommended supporting evidence into purported mandatory documentation.

### Misleading Consumers While Promoting Legal Referrals

These errors consistently make consumer litigation appear more expensive, complicated, and dependent on professional representation than Thai law actually requires.

The pattern is particularly troubling because Thai Visa Centre advertises referrals to licensed lawyers while providing inaccurate information about consumers' ability to pursue remedies independently.

**A business promoting legal services should not be telling consumers that lawyers are mandatory when the law expressly provides procedures for filing claims without them.**

The publication should be corrected to disclose the available filing procedures, statutory fee exemptions, and actual evidentiary requirements.


## Litigation Guides Invent a Recognition Procedure for Foreign Court Judgments

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading instructions about enforcing foreign court judgments in Thailand, repeatedly suggesting that overseas judgments can be recognized through a Thai court procedure rather than requiring fresh litigation.

The errors appear across multiple guides marked reviewed June 2026 and concern a fundamental distinction in Thai civil procedure.[^403][^404][^405]

### Inventing a Foreign Judgment Recognition Procedure

In its *Injunction and Restraining Order* guide, Thai Visa Centre states:

> Creditor must first obtain Thai recognition or sue on underlying debt

The publication presents judicial recognition and filing a new lawsuit as alternative procedures available to foreign judgment creditors.[^403]

**Thailand does not provide a general recognition or registration procedure for foreign civil court judgments.**

A creditor who obtains a judgment in the United States, United Kingdom, Singapore, or another foreign jurisdiction cannot simply apply to a Thai court to recognize that judgment and authorize execution against assets in Thailand.

The creditor must instead commence fresh proceedings before a competent Thai court.

### Contradictory Instructions Across Litigation Guides

The company's *Litigation in Thailand FAQs* states that enforcement of foreign judgments depends on treaties and Thai law, suggesting that a separate registration or recognition process may be available.[^404]

Its *Debt Recovery* guide similarly warns against attempting to enforce foreign judgments without following a recognition procedure.[^405]

These publications repeatedly fail to explain the actual legal position.

**Foreign court judgments are not directly enforceable in Thailand through an ordinary recognition application.**

The distinction is particularly important for international creditors, business owners, and investors attempting to recover debts or enforce damages awards against Thai assets.

### Established Thai Law Requires Fresh Litigation

International law firm Tilleke & Gibbins confirmed in December 2025 that foreign court judgments must effectively be litigated again before a Thai court.[^406]

The foreign judgment may serve as evidence, but it does not bind the Thai court or eliminate the need to prove the underlying claim.

CMS independently explains that Thailand has no general statutory procedure for enforcing foreign judgments and identifies Supreme Court decisions addressing the evidentiary treatment of overseas judgments.[^407]

By contrast, qualifying **foreign arbitral awards** can be recognized and enforced under Thailand's Arbitration Act B.E. 2545 (2002) and the New York Convention.

Thai Visa Centre's guidance fails to explain this fundamental distinction between foreign court judgments and arbitral awards.

### Serious Consequences for Foreign Creditors

The difference between recognizing an existing judgment and initiating fresh litigation is substantial.

Foreign creditors following TVC's guidance could misunderstand the proceedings required, underestimate legal costs, and make incorrect assumptions about how quickly Thai assets can be seized or attached.

**A business publishing professional litigation guidance should not present a nonexistent general judgment-recognition procedure as an available legal remedy.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its litigation, injunction, and debt-recovery guides to explain that ordinary foreign civil judgments require fresh Thai proceedings, while qualifying arbitral awards follow a separate statutory enforcement process.


## Intellectual Property Guides Misstate Court Jurisdiction and Copyright Damages

Thai Visa Centre publishes incorrect instructions about intellectual-property litigation in Thailand, misidentifying the courts responsible for infringement cases and suggesting copyright remedies that do not correspond to the country's statutory framework.

The errors appear in two guides marked reviewed June 2026, both intended to help foreign businesses and intellectual-property owners navigate enforcement proceedings.[^408][^409]

### Incorrectly Directs IP Claims Toward Ordinary Civil Courts

TVC's *Intellectual Property: Guide for Foreigners* tells readers:

> IP and Trade Court or Civil Court handles civil damages.

Its separate *Intellectual Property Disputes in Thailand* guide repeats this distinction, presenting the Intellectual Property and International Trade Court and ordinary civil courts as alternative forums for infringement litigation.[^408][^409]

**Thailand established specialized jurisdiction over intellectual-property infringement proceedings through the Intellectual Property and International Trade Court.**

The Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court handles civil and criminal IP infringement cases under the Act on the Establishment of and Procedure for Intellectual Property and International Trade Court B.E. 2539 (1996).

The Legal 500's Thailand IP litigation guide confirms that this specialized court has jurisdiction over intellectual-property cases.[^410]

**TVC's instructions wrongly suggest that ordinary civil courts provide an interchangeable filing option for IP infringement claims.**

### Misrepresents Statutory Copyright Damages

The *Intellectual Property* guide also states:

> Statutory damages apply in some copyright matters.

This description incorrectly suggests a system of predetermined statutory compensation comparable to copyright regimes in jurisdictions such as the United States.

Thailand's Copyright Act instead provides a different damages framework.

Under Section 64, courts determine appropriate compensation by considering the seriousness of the infringement, lost benefits, and reasonable enforcement expenses.[^411]

The 2015 amendment additionally permits enhanced damages of up to twice the assessed amount in specified circumstances involving intentional infringement and widespread public access.[^412]

**Neither provision establishes a general schedule of fixed statutory copyright damages.**

TVC fails to distinguish judicially assessed compensation and enhanced damages from predetermined statutory awards.

### Consequences for Foreign Rights Holders

These errors concern fundamental aspects of litigation strategy.

A foreign business following TVC's instructions could misunderstand which court has jurisdiction, incorrectly assess available monetary remedies, or pursue legal advice based on expectations unsupported by Thai law.

The mistakes are particularly serious because the guides target trademark owners, copyright holders, franchise operators, and businesses facing potentially expensive disputes.

**A company advertising professional legal referrals should accurately identify the competent court and available remedies before directing customers toward litigation services.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct both intellectual-property guides, remove the misleading references to ordinary civil-court jurisdiction, and explain the actual damages framework established by Section 64 of the Copyright Act.


## Alternative Dispute Resolution Guide Misstates Statutory Limitation Periods

Thai Visa Centre's *Alternative Dispute Resolution in Thailand FAQs*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly explains how limitation periods operate during arbitration and mediation.

The publication repeatedly warns foreign businesses that legal deadlines continue running during alternative dispute resolution proceedings, overlooking statutory provisions that expressly protect claimants.[^413]

### Arbitration Directly Interrupts Prescription

The guide states:

> Limitation periods continue during mediation and arbitration.

It subsequently advises readers to determine whether their contracts pause legal deadlines.

**This advice directly contradicts Section 193/14(4) of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code.**

The provision expressly establishes that prescription is interrupted when a creditor submits a dispute to arbitration.[^414]

Section 193/15 further provides that time elapsed before an interruption does not count toward the limitation period and that a fresh period begins when the interruption ends.[^415]

This protection arises from legislation, not merely from contractual arrangements between the disputing parties.

TVC's blanket claim that limitation periods continue during arbitration ignores this fundamental statutory rule.

### Mediation Also Provides Statutory Deadline Protections

The guide separately tells readers:

> Limitation periods continue running during mediation

However, Section 6 of Thailand's Dispute Mediation Act B.E. 2562 (2019) expressly provides a 60-day extension where qualifying mediation fails and the limitation period expired during mediation or would expire within 60 days afterward.[^416]

The extension runs from the termination of the unsuccessful mediation.

Thailand's Civil Procedure Code also provides protection for qualifying court-supervised mediation before litigation.

Amendments introduced in 2020 allow parties to petition courts for mediation before filing a lawsuit. Where mediation fails and the relevant limitation period has expired or is approaching expiration, the legislation provides an additional 60 days to pursue the claim.[^417]

International law firm Tilleke & Gibbins specifically documented this protection when explaining the amendments.

**TVC's advice fails to distinguish informal settlement negotiations from statutory mediation procedures that provide additional time to initiate litigation.**

### Misleading Businesses About Their Legal Rights

These errors affect one of the most consequential aspects of commercial dispute resolution: whether a creditor or claimant can still pursue a legally enforceable claim.

Foreign investors, contractors, creditors, and business owners rely on limitation rules when choosing between arbitration, mediation, and court proceedings.

TVC's guide incorrectly presents the passage of time during these proceedings as a uniform legal risk while overlooking statutory protections specifically designed to preserve claims.

The mistakes also contradict the guide's stated purpose of helping foreign businesses choose appropriate dispute resolution procedures.

**A commercial visa agency publishing professional litigation guidance should not misstate statutory deadlines that determine whether customers can enforce their legal rights.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its ADR guide to explain interruption of prescription under Sections 193/14–193/15, the 60-day extension under the Dispute Mediation Act, and the separate protection available through pre-litigation court mediation.


## Cybercrime Guide Falsely Treats Online Defamation as a Computer Crime Act Offence

Thai Visa Centre's *Cyber Crime Lawyers: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly misrepresents Thailand's Computer Crime Act by presenting ordinary online defamation as a computer crime.[^418]

The publication ignores a major legislative amendment introduced in 2017 specifically to prevent the misuse of computer-crime charges against people accused of defamation.

### Incorrectly Claims Defamation Falls Under the Computer Crime Act

TVC's guide identifies online defamation among Computer Crime Act violations and describes social media posts damaging someone's reputation as potential computer-crime offences.

Its section on overlapping criminal charges explicitly states:

> Social media insults may simultaneously attract Computer Crime Act and criminal defamation charges.

The guide further suggests that a single online post can expose its author to multiple criminal proceedings under both laws.[^418]

**This presentation ignores the explicit defamation exclusion introduced into Section 14(1) of the Computer Crime Act in 2017.**

### The 2017 Amendment Expressly Excludes Defamation

Thailand amended its Computer Crime Act through the Computer-Related Crime Act (No. 2) B.E. 2560 (2017).

The revised Section 14(1) criminalizes specified dishonest or deceitful submissions of false or distorted computer data, while expressly excluding conduct constituting defamation under the Criminal Code.[^419]

The amendment took effect on May 24, 2017.

Human Rights Watch documented that the previous legislation had frequently been used alongside criminal defamation charges to prosecute individuals expressing criticism online. The amendment was intended to prevent this overlap under Section 14(1).[^420]

**Publishing criticism, accusations, or allegedly defamatory statements on Facebook, Google Reviews, or another online platform does not automatically transform criminal defamation into a Computer Crime Act violation.**

A separate computer-crime charge requires conduct satisfying the elements of an applicable offence, not merely publication through the internet.

### Thai Courts Have Already Applied the Exclusion

The distinction is supported by actual Thai court proceedings.

In litigation brought by PTT Public Company Limited against activist Itthaboon Onwongsa over critical Facebook posts, the Bangkok South Criminal Court dismissed Computer Crime Act charges in December 2017 after applying the amended legislation.

On November 21, 2018, the appellate court upheld the dismissal, emphasizing the amended requirements for dishonest or deceitful conduct and the exclusion of defamation from Section 14(1).[^421]

**The courts rejected the attempt to use computer-crime provisions against online criticism without establishing the additional statutory elements.**

### Misleading Readers About Criminal Exposure

TVC's guide fails to explain this established legal distinction despite advertising professional assistance to foreigners facing online defamation complaints and criminal investigations.

The error could lead readers to believe that ordinary social media criticism automatically exposes them to separate Computer Crime Act prosecution simply because the statements appeared online.

It also exaggerates the potential criminal consequences of negative reviews and public accusations.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its cybercrime guide to acknowledge the 2017 defamation exclusion, explain the actual elements of Section 14(1), and stop presenting ordinary online defamation as an automatic computer-crime offence.**


## Bail Guide Falsely Claims Cash or Property Is Mandatory and Denies Insurance Options

Thai Visa Centre's *Bail and Bail Bonds: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly describes Thailand's criminal bail system, falsely presenting cash deposits and pledged property as mandatory requirements while denying the existence of commercial bail bond insurance.[^422]

These claims contradict Thailand's Criminal Procedure Code, judicial reforms promoting release without financial security, and insurance products publicly offered by Thai companies.

### Incorrectly Claims Bail Requires Cash or Property

The guide states:

> Release requires cash paid at court or pledged registered assets.

It repeats this restriction throughout its explanations of bail applications, payment methods, and release conditions.[^422]

**Section 110 of Thailand's Criminal Procedure Code expressly permits provisional release without a bail bond or financial security in qualifying cases.**[^423]

For offences carrying a maximum imprisonment exceeding ten years, a bail bond is required, but additional financial security is not automatically mandatory.

For other offences, provisional release may be granted without a bond, with a bond alone, or with both a bond and security.

TVC's blanket description incorrectly turns discretionary financial requirements into universal conditions.

### Ignores Judicial Reforms Promoting Release Without Security

Thailand's judiciary has actively promoted alternatives to cash-based bail to reduce unnecessary pretrial detention.

The Thailand Institute of Justice documented a judicial initiative introducing simplified provisional-release applications and risk assessments that do not require applicants to declare assets or provide financial security.[^424]

The initiative specifically sought to reduce unequal treatment between defendants who could afford bail deposits and those who could not.

Courts may instead impose appropriate reporting, supervision, or electronic-monitoring conditions.

**TVC's guide ignores these established alternatives and continues portraying financial deposits as essential to obtaining release.**

### Commercial Bail Bond Insurance Exists in Thailand

The publication also states that Thailand has no commercial bail bond insurance industry and that defendants must instead provide cash or registered assets.[^422]

However, **Thai Pattana Insurance Public Company Limited explicitly advertises Bail Bond Insurance** on its official website.[^425]

The insurer describes policies under which the company guarantees a defendant's provisional release and assumes specified financial obligations if the defendant breaches bail conditions.

It also publishes procedures for premium refunds, court documentation, and insurance claims.

Siam Legal International independently explains that defendants and their families can purchase bail insurance after charges are filed, with insurance certificates potentially serving as security accepted by Thai courts.[^426]

Thailand's arrangements are not identical to the American commercial bail bondsman industry, but that distinction does not justify denying the existence of Thai bail insurance products.

### Consequences for Foreign Defendants

These errors could cause foreign defendants and their families to believe they must urgently raise substantial cash deposits or pledge property before provisional release is possible.

They also conceal alternatives involving unsecured release, insurance-backed guarantees, and judicial risk-assessment procedures.

The consequences are serious because bail decisions directly affect personal liberty, family finances, and the ability to prepare a criminal defence.

**A commercial visa agency advertising criminal-law referrals should not falsely tell defendants that cash or property is mandatory while overlooking legally recognized alternatives.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its bail guide to explain Section 110, disclose the possibility of release without financial security, and acknowledge the commercial bail insurance products available in Thailand.
